Munger's Verdict

MUNGER SYNTHESIS — Aecon Group Inc. (TSX: ARE) · REVISION 5 (FINAL)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Price: CAD 43.55 · All figures CAD Fifth and final. Nine FATAL findings across four red-team passes; all conceded and acted on. Pass 4 found one — a false universal in the sentence I nominated as the verdict's foundation — and found me CAD 1.33 too punitive on my own load-bearing dial. Both are fixed below. The verdict does not change. The margin behind it does: from 6% to 3.2%, and that belongs in the headline rather than a footnote.**

PRELIMINARY: THE LEDGER

The standing charge across four passes is that I concede in prose and leave the dollars. Revision 3 answered with a ledger whose arithmetic checked and whose currency did not — two credits priced inside the EV/EBIT method the next line abolished. Re-denominated in the method I publish (multiple × normalised EPS), reconciling to the published ceiling:

step ceiling
Revision 2 as published (13x EV/EBIT × EBIT 214 − net debt 334) CAD 35.72
FATAL-C/F — restate the same earnings in the published method (16.5x × EPS 1.81) 29.91 · −5.81
FATAL-A/E — EBIT 214 → 233.8 (drop the double haircut, correct the 2025 overhead basis, raise revenue). The bear-ward leg of FATAL-E — bull EBIT 310 → 302 — is not visible here; it lands in the line below, which shrank from +1.20 to +0.82. 33.41 · +3.50
FATAL-D — debt basis 334 → 174.4 33.41 · +0.00
self-introduced — scenario-weight the EPS 34.24 · +0.83
SERIOUS-P4-1 — apply the midpoint rule to g₁ as well as to the margin 35.00 · +0.76
Final action level CAD 35

The +CAD 2.33 debt credit I booked in Revision 3 is worth exactly zero and I withdrew it. Net debt does not enter 16.5 × EPS; it enters only through finance cost, which is set from an observed run-rate independent of which basis is chosen. My response to the four FATAL findings is CAD −2.31, plus CAD 0.83 I introduced on my own initiative and CAD 0.76 the gate found in my favour. Every line ties to a change in the text below.

FATAL-E — THE Q1 PRINT IS REAL. "FIXED" IS NOT.

The gate verified the citation at initial_dossier line 268 — Q1 2026 gross profit CAD 100.5M against consolidated revenue of 1,257.0 — and formally withdrew pass 2's charge that I invented CAD 185M. That limb stands. The second limb does not, and the falsification is one line I never ran:

Q1 2026 overhead = gross profit 100.5 − operating profit 34.1 = CAD  66.4M
Q2 2026 overhead = gross profit 154.7 − operating profit 36.4 = CAD 118.3M

A "fixed" cost does not rise 78% in ninety days. Worse, run my own model backwards: at F = 92.35/quarter, Q1 gross profit would have had to be CAD 126.4M, and the filed figure is 100.5. The line I cited to prove the number was filed is the line that refutes the model that uses it. The sentence "F = 184.7 is a filed number, not an assumption, and it resolves the reconciliation gap" is struck.

Two further concessions. My segment rebuttal answered a question the dossier never asked. The warning was never about Concessions revenue being 0.1% of the total; it was about ~CAD 23M/quarter of corporate cost and eliminations — and the dossier names "deferral of intercompany construction profit on network assets under development" in this very quarter, which is a cost-of-revenue item. The reconciliation gap is papered over, not resolved. And my corroborating decomposition double-counted: MG&A at 4.1% is the consolidated line and already contains corporate cost, so adding "corporate ≈ 46" on top counts it twice, and the third term was defined as the remainder. Three terms, one a plug, summing to the target by construction. That is the CHECK-0 error I have now committed in three consecutive drafts. Withdrawn.

Published as a range, as instructed — and here is the mitigation I am entitled to. The alternative build holds Q1's implied overhead constant, giving F ≈ CAD 132.8/half and an H1 gross margin of 7.04%:

FY2026 consolidated operating margin F = 184.7 F = 132.8
no step (bear) — F fixed / F proportional 2.97% / 2.44% 2.82% / 2.44%
half step (central) 3.63–3.97% / 3.51–3.56% 3.49–3.74% / 3.51–3.56%
full step (bull) 4.30–4.67% / 4.59–4.68% 4.16–4.43% / 4.59–4.68%

The F question is load-bearing under only one of the two overhead treatments. The proportional treatment reduces algebraically to the raw operating-margin step and needs no F at all — and it sits at the top of the bull range (4.68%) and the bottom of the bear range (2.44%) under both values. So the honest ceiling is 4.16–4.68%, against Revision 3's 4.30–4.68%, and the honest central is 3.49–3.97%, essentially unchanged. The claim was wrong; the answer moves CAD 0.09 of EPS. I state the sign, which Revision 3 did not: F was bear-ward in Revision 2 and bull-ward in Revision 3, and correcting it costs the bull case, not the bear.

FY2026 EBIT, midpoint of each published range — no longer hand-picked points: bear CAD 170M (2.70%), central CAD 234M (3.64%), bull CAD 302M (4.56%).

Revenue endpoints unchanged and both published: CAD 6,292M at +11% H2 and CAD 6,635M at H1's actual +22.2%, centred at +15% and labelled a judgment.

And the narrowed thesis sentence stands, because it is still true: the best consolidated operating margin ever printed in this record is 3.33% for one half and 1.60% for a full year. FY2024 was −1.43%, H1 2026 was 2.44%. A full-year margin above 4% has never been printed here on any basis.

FATAL-F — ONE DIAL, NOT TWO WITNESSES. AND THE INSTRUCTION I NEVER NAMED.

This is the important one and the gate is right about all of it.

I claimed the multiple was derived twice — from the tribunal's severity count (15–18x) and from a two-stage capitalisation at the mandated 10–12% hurdle (15.9–18.4x) — and ran a symbolic check to prove independence. The check tested whether the two formulas share a symbol. That is not the question. The question is whether g₁ was determined by anything other than the answer it had to produce. It was not. Anchor 2 is a crank, not a witness, and the proof is that turning it to zero reproduces Anchor 1's other end: at g₁ = 0% it prints CAD 21.63 ≈ 10.4x, which is the "absurdly cheap" floor. A model that reproduces the other anchor at both ends is a calibration instrument. "Two witnesses" and "the first multiple I can hand a reader with a derivation attached" are struck.

So here is the whole memo on one page. Normalised earnings E₀ = CAD 142.2M (scenario-weighted), k = 11% (dossier midpoint), ten years at g₁, then 3% terminal:

g₁ CAD/share implied P/E what it is
0% 21.63 10.4x the dossier's mandated g on its stated inputs — my floor
3% 26.71 12.9x
4.1% 28.90 13.9x the top-risk case — and see the witness below
6% 33.09 15.9x bottom of my action band
6.5% 34.30 16.5x midpoint of the multiple window
7% 35.56 17.1x midpoint of the growth window — published action level CAD 35
8% 38.21 18.4x top of my action band
9.4% 42.15 20.3x the mandated formula on consistent normalised inputs
9.8% 43.55 21.0x today's price

The published buy zone is not "10x to 16.5x." It is g₁ from −0.6% to 8%, with spot at 9.8%. Four labels, one dial. Disclosed.

SERIOUS-P4-1 conceded, and it is the one finding in four passes that runs in my favour. I imposed a midpoint rule on every margin cell in this document and then declined it on Load-Bearing Factor #1. My two stated windows for the same dial do not coincide — the tribunal's 15–18x maps to g₁ 5.1–7.7%, while my stated 6–8% maps to 16.0–18.4x — and I computed off the midpoint of the multiple window (16.5x → CAD 34.24) while ignoring the midpoint of the growth window (7% → CAD 35.56). Same rule, two answers, CAD 1.32 apart, and I took the lower without saying the other existed. There is no principled reason to prefer midpointing in multiple-space over growth-space; the map between them is non-linear, so the disagreement is the precision available. I therefore publish the action level as the centre of the band my own window produces — g₁ 6–8% → CAD 33–38 → action level CAD 35 — rather than either point estimate. That is bull-ward by CAD 0.76 and it is the correct reading of my own "Low precision" confession: naming a level to two decimals off a dial I have just told the reader is unresolved is the false precision this framework exists to catch.

And the second witness I already owned and did not claim. Run the dossier's mandated identity on my bear EPS of CAD 1.342: g = (1.342 − 0.77)/13.93 = 4.10%. I had independently judged the top-risk growth rate at 4.0%, reasoning from nominal GDP plus a cycle and from new awards down 46% — a route that shares nothing with the identity except the earnings figure, with book value and dividend taken from the dossier. Two different instruments returning 4.10% and 4.0% on the load-bearing dial. The values that follow (CAD 28.90 and CAD 28.68) agree by construction because both discount the same E₀, so the witness is on the growth rate, not on the price — I say that explicitly, because manufacturing a convergence out of a shared denominator is the error I have been convicted of three times. On the growth rate it is real, and it is the first genuine second witness in five drafts.

Discharging the mandatory instruction

The dossier carries exactly one instruction marked MANDATORY, and it governs precisely this input: "Sustainable growth g = ROE × retention = −0.0%. Any valuation that assumes a growth rate must justify departing from this figure… any expert using a positive growth rate must justify the departure explicitly and say what they are normalising ROE to." Three drafts and I never named it. Named now, and discharged on its own terms rather than around them.

What I am normalising ROE to, as instructed. The mandated −0.0% is the product of two contaminated inputs, and the dossier says so itself in the same block: ROE of −7.0% is driven by the CAD 128M non-cash Aecon Utilities charge, and retention of 0% comes from a 139% payout computed on a TTM EPS of −0.95. Run the dossier's own formula on normalised inputs:

g = ROE × retention = (E/B) × (1 − D/E) ≡ (E − D)/B
  E = CAD 2.0748   weighted normalised EPS — the same base my valuation discounts
  B = CAD 13.93    book value/share  [dossier]
  D = CAD 0.77     dividend/share    [dossier]
  → ROE 14.90% × retention 62.89% = g = 9.37%   → fair value CAD 42.15

Note what is and is not chosen here. The share count cancels out entirely; book value and dividend are the dossier's own figures; the only input from my model is the earnings figure. And the CAD 128M charge does not contaminate the numerator — it contaminates the denominator, because it depressed book equity, which inflates ROE and therefore inflates g. Since I deploy this as the most bull-ward reading of the mandated instrument and then argue it still falls short, the contamination makes my own argument harder, not easier.

The dossier's own mandated formula spans −0.0% to 9.37% depending on which inputs you feed it — a fair-value span of CAD 21.63 to CAD 42.15. My CAD 35 sits inside that span, not outside it. That is the honest statement, and it is materially less flattering to me than "derived twice."

Why I sit at 6–8% rather than at 9%. The identity g = ROE × retention assumes growth is funded by retained earnings earning ROE. Aecon issued CAD 172.5M of equity in March 2026 at a discount to fund growth — retained earnings were demonstrably not sufficient, which falsifies the identity's premise in the direction of requiring more capital per unit of growth, not less. The 14.54% ROE is struck on a book equity depressed by successive loss years, against normalised earnings the company has never printed. The 62% retention assumes a dividend frozen at CAD 0.77 while earnings sextuple, and management just raised it. And the record: revenue went CAD 4.6B → 4.2B → 5.4B, a 5.5% three-year CAGR.

Why I do not sit at 0%. Because — and SERIOUS-P3-6 conceded, this is a bull fact in the file that I dropped — management guided double-digit revenue growth in 2026 and further growth in 2027 [TRANSCRIPT per Q2 2026 earnings call — a transcript, not a filing, and I mistagged it [SEC] last round], the only multi-year forward statement in the record, and I never cited it in three drafts. Alongside it: Ontario's CAD 37B 2026–27 capital budget, a named CAD 1.7B Q3 backlog addition plus a separate 33.3%-share award, Darlington site prep on time and on budget, and H1 2026 revenue up 22.2%. "A growth rate nothing in this file supports" was a factual claim about the file and it was false. Withdrawn — the same failure mode as Revision 2's over-concession, in the opposite direction, and I should have seen it coming.

Against all of that: backlog down 2.4% YoY, new awards down 46% YoY — the flow, not the stock — and the Canada Infrastructure Bank on record as not expected to reach its disbursement goals in any sector by 2027-28. Double-digit revenue growth for two years is not ten years of 10% earnings growth.

Council witnesses on the same window, correctly attributed. The Biologist — not the Futurist, as I wrote last round on a citation I inherited without checking — arrives at "nominal GDP-plus-infrastructure-cycle growth (~6-8%)" by his own route and publishes a trigger of CAD 32–37, which brackets my CAD 35. He is a weak witness: his stated basis is halving the pipeline's 15% growth assumption, a number the dossier instructs us to ignore. Separately, the Futurist publishes a sub-spot trigger of CAD 35 on an 11% hurdle by a different instrument again. Two weak real witnesses are worth more than one strong fabricated one — and putting the wrong name on the only outside witness I cite is not a small thing in a memo whose best quality across five drafts has been refusing to attribute what it cannot source.

Load-Bearing Factor #1 is now g₁, named as such, and the reader who believes 9–10% is right should conclude the stock is roughly fairly valued and take no position. That is a coherent view, it is close to what the dossier's own formula gives on normalised inputs, and I will not caricature it.

FATAL-D — THE CONCLUSION STANDS. THE "IDENTITY" DOES NOT PROVE IT.

SERIOUS-P3-1 conceded. 804.0 − 309.6 = 494.4 under the lease reading; the two bases agree at CAD 174.4M only if you have already assumed the CAD 320M inside totalDebt is the Oaktree preferred. Observing that they agree therefore restates the assumption — affirming the consequent, the same class of error as the fabricated reconciliation it replaced. "That is what a reconciliation looks like" is struck.

The conclusion stands on affirmative evidence alone: the CAD 320.0M gap matches the preferred's marked carrying value to the decimal; the CAD 128M fair-value adjustment was recorded in finance cost, which is how a redeemable preferred classified as a financial liability behaves; and financial liabilities are what data vendors sweep into totalDebt. Debt basis stated: CAD 484.0M [SEC balance sheet], with the CAD 320M Oaktree outflow deducted from cash → pro-forma net debt CAD 174.4M. Under the dossier's unsourced "most likely leases" reading it is CAD 494.4M. Both published; and net debt is inert in my method, so the unresolved defect touches the band only through finance cost.

SERIOUS-P3-2 conceded — I took the balance-sheet limb and left the income-statement limb unspoken. Taking both, and sizing them:

  • Credit: retiring a CAD 320M financial liability removes its carry. Oaktree turned CAD 150.0M net into CAD 320M over ~32 months; the terms are a named blind spot, but the carry was not zero. Relief: CAD 10–20M/yr.
  • Charge: the CAD 320M of cash used to retire it stops earning. At 3.5% that is CAD 11M/yr — and a P/E method must charge it, because net debt does not enter anywhere else.

SERIOUS-P4-2 conceded — my own two legs did not close. Relief of 10–20 less a charge of 11 is a net of −1 to +9, not the "−7 to +9" I printed, and it gives a post-Oaktree finance-cost range of CAD 36–46M, not the 30–45 I published. The CAD 30M floor required CAD 6M more relief than my own top-of-range sizing allowed, and the CAD 2.65/share sensitivity I offered the reader was therefore about CAD 1.06 too generous. Corrected: the range is CAD 36–46M and the sensitivity is CAD 1.59/share at the bottom, CAD 0.71 at the midpoint of 41.

I hold finance cost at CAD 45M and I do not midpoint it — and here is the rule, because I have just midpointed everything else. I take midpoints where both endpoints are derived from filed prints (every margin cell, every scenario EBIT) and where both endpoints are judgments (g₁). I anchor to the filed print where one exists and the range around it is built on a dossier-named blind spot — and this is that case: CAD 45.6M/yr is the observed underlying run-rate [SEC, Q2 2026 ex-charge], while the relief leg depends on the Oaktree preferred's contractual return, which the dossier names as a blind spot and I still decline to assert. I prefer a filed number to the midpoint of an undisclosed one, and I state what it costs a reader who disagrees: CAD 0.71 to CAD 1.59/share. Named, sized, and not silently declined.

MOAT TRIBUNAL

Moat Tribunal: 0/5 SEVERE. 5/5 MODERATE — Buffett, Biologist, Historian, Anthropologist, Psychologist. 0 MINOR/NONE. No Moat Uncertainty Discount. No Casualty Trajectory above 60%. Franchise Premium fails all four criteria (operating margins 2–5%, not >35%).

Verdict: narrow, cyclical, unproven above cost of capital. Real: 25–33% equity in two 30-year P3 concessions, a CNSC-licensed seat in the Darlington alliance, and a demonstrated sovereign veto — Canada blocked CCCI's CAD 1.51B bid on national-security grounds, which caps the exit and fences the CNSC-licensed constructor set. Both signs, booked. Real limits: 99.9% of revenue is re-bid every procurement; ROIC cleared the 10–12% hurdle in one of four clean years, and that year produced negative owner earnings.

Where the fence gets paid for: it is why the tribunal returns MODERATE rather than SEVERE, and MODERATE is what puts the ceiling at 15–18x rather than the 10x Graham case — worth CAD 13.48/share on weighted EPS. Paid once, not twice.

BULL-BEAR BALANCE

Every bull case as filed. Bezos/Buffett/Cook/Jobs/Psychologist: four quarters near Q2's 5.1% Adjusted EBITDA plus normalised receivables. Burry: legacy losses vanish and 5% sticks. Sherlock: a genuine nuclear/collaborative mix-shift, concessions begin compounding. Futurist and Biologist: Ontario's CAD 37B capital budget and a nuclear buildout so far on time and on budget. Historian: the SNC-Lavalin/AtkinsRéalis pivot from fixed-price casualty to collaborative survivor. Anthropologist: a gatekept tribe. Lynch: legacy overhang shed as nuclear ramps.

Lynch — correction stands, verified three times. H2 2025 operating profit alone was CAD 102.1M, so his residual "H2 2025 produced CAD 57.3M of EBITDA" is arithmetically impossible. But his conclusion — that the trailing multiple prices a company that no longer exists — survives on seasonality he never computed and I refused to compute until pass 1 made me.

SERIOUS-P3-5 conceded — I published three bull weights and computed with one. A memo that announces 55% and calculates on 30% has not raised its bull weight. The 55% and 35% are withdrawn. One set, used everywhere: bear 25% / central 45% / bull 30% — which is the two-camp split the framework asks for at bulls 30%, non-bulls 70%. These are the numbers that produce weighted EPS of CAD 2.07 and therefore the entire buy zone. The observation behind the withdrawn pair survives as prose and nothing more: the bull case on FY2026 earnings is stronger than the bull case on value at CAD 43.55, because the margin is not what has to be right — g₁ is.

SERIOUS-1 (pass 2) stands conceded. The CAD 1.7B Q3 addition is a lump added to a stock; the bear signal is new awards down 46% YoY, a flow, and one contract establishes no run-rate. The definitional-artifact argument cuts both ways: if the reported series excludes collaborative and progressive awards, the CAD 1.7B may be recognition of work already won rather than new demand. Backlog is not a factor that moves fair value — it is the file's only forward instrument, and it is the principal evidence against g₁ at 9–10%.

Private Buyer Test — demoted and it stays demoted. It runs off the same EBIT and is not an independent valuation. The one genuinely independent datum is the CCCI bid: a filed CAD 1.51B offer, blocked, with no earnings figure retrieved alongside it, so it yields no multiple. It says a strategic buyer existed at scale and the highest-bidding class is now legally excluded. Both facts, both directions, no multiple — and no peer band anywhere in this document.

RISKS, RE-SIZED

The top risk, in dollars, on a single base. Today's price is the model at g₁ = 9.8%. If decade earnings growth is 4% instead — nominal GDP plus a cycle, which is what backlog down 2.4% and new awards down 46% point to — the equity is worth CAD 28.68. That is a permanent loss of CAD 14.87/share, 34% of today's price, with no accounting event required. Properly weighted across all three earnings scenarios at g₁ = 4% it is 33%. Everything else here is smaller.

Receivables. AR/revenue broke a 12–14% band (13.6% / 12.9% / 11.9%) to 17.7%. Real signal. Range on the defensible norms: CAD 221–260M, central CAD 241M (CAD 3.52/share) — the trough-base endpoint pass 1 struck stays struck. Contaminants I cannot strip: mismatched windows, two 2025 US acquisitions named but not sized, and no billed/unbilled split. No recovery-rate haircut is assumable — Coastal GasLink settled non-cash, with the company stating it would not affect cash flow. Carrying-value risk, not a liquidity event, and not deducted from my value.

Liquidity. CAD 320M to Oaktree, closing Q4 2026, against CAD 629.6M — 51%, in the cash-generative half.

The CAD 224M cash-flow residual — rebuttal upheld by the gate. TTM OCF 368 − TTM normalised NI 18 − D&amp;A 106 − SBC 20 = 224, same period throughout, with normalised NI of ~CAD 18M after adding back the CAD 128M non-cash charge. Pass 2's CAD 129M substituted forward income into a trailing bridge. Composition undisclosed.

SERIOUS-P3-7 conceded — I discarded the one observed route for the wrong stated reason. TTM FCF of CAD 304M at the mandated 10–12% gives CAD 36.97–44.36; TTM owner earnings of CAD 210M gives CAD 25.54–30.64. I declined to let either touch the band on the ground that neither is independent of the earnings route. That reasoning was too strong. Both capitalise a stream, but one capitalises an observed trailing cash flow while the other capitalises a forecast EPS from a margin model with a contested overhead parameter. Those are not the same witness. The honest statement: the FCF route spans CAD 25.5–44.4, it substantially overlaps my published CAD 21–34, its midpoint sits near my ceiling, and it mildly corroborates rather than contradicts. What it cannot do is narrow anything, because the CAD 14/share gap between its two ends is the undisclosed-residual question.

Stress test — withdrawn, and I restate why. The dossier's table prints Base revenue CAD 5.43B with Adjusted FCF turning negative at roughly −10%, on a base the dossier declares defective (a 48% simple-FCF margin). My reconstructions give −25% under one overhead treatment and no volume breakeven at all under the other. Three answers means the instrument measures nothing here. The real fragility is single-project impairment — and SERIOUS-P4-4 conceded, I sized it with the wrong company's number. The CAD 108M and CAD 1.58 I printed are the filed Q2 2026 loss attributable to shareholders and its per-share figure, driven by the CAD 128M non-cash fair-value adjustment on the Oaktree preferred — a financing item I spend two sections of this memo adding back as non-recurring. I scared the reader with the charge I had just quarantined. Correctly sized: a repeat of FY2024's CAD 60.1M impairment-driven operating loss costs CAD 44M after tax, CAD 0.64 of EPS — 2.5x smaller than I claimed. And no stress test in this file measures it.

Recurrence risk is charged once, in the multiple, by staying at the tribunal's normal 15–18x rather than raising the ceiling. The margin haircut is gone and stays gone.

Insiders — MINOR-P3-2 corrected. Lyash is the most recent insider purchase, not the most recent transaction: Nackan sold 1,000 on 2026-08-06 and 1,000 on 2026-08-18, both after. Sells: Rosenfeld 125,000 @ 41.64 on 27-Mar-2026, the offering's closing day — 25.7% of the entire ~487,000-share insider register; Beck 20,000 @ 49.77; Murphy 2,930 @ 52.31; Chan 521 @ 55.09; Nackan 2,000. Buys: Lyash (non-exec director), 31-Jul-2026, at roughly spot — above my entire buy zone. Two feeds conflict; per instruction I resolve neither and size both on the same basis: 3,184 shares = 0.65%; 41,796 shares = 8.6% of the register. I previously took price from one feed and share count from the other, and said so.

Sherlock's sequence survives a fourth pass: equity issued at a CAD 1.57 discount, dividend raised the same quarter on a 139% payout, CAD 320M committed within six months. Three things financed with two.

FINAL DECISION

Decision: WAIT — and it survives by about 3%, not by 6%. That belongs in the first line, not a footnote.

FATAL-G conceded, and it is the sentence I nominated as the verdict's foundation. I wrote that "no reading of the dossier's mandated growth formula, on any inputs, produces a fair value above CAD 41." False — and false because I fed the formula my central EPS of 2.025 and then discounted the answer it produced off E₀ = 142.2, which is my weighted EPS of 2.075. Two earnings bases inside one calculation, three sections after I convicted myself of exactly this on "20.7x → 10.1%" and fixed it. Put both legs on the base my valuation actually uses:

E = 2.0748 (weighted normalised EPS)
ROE       = 2.0748 / 13.93        = 14.90%
retention = 1 − 0.77 / 2.0748     = 62.89%
g         = ROE × retention       =  9.37%
fair value at g₁ = 9.37%          = CAD 42.15

The word "any" is struck. Readings above CAD 41 exist, and I do not have to strain to find one — the more internally consistent of the two available inputs produces it. Readings that clear spot also exist: applied per-scenario, since ROE and payout are state-dependent, and weighted at my own 25/45/30, the identity returns CAD 44.25. I do not adopt that one and I will not pretend it flips the verdict — it stacks a bull-scenario ROE into a ten-year growth rate and a bull earnings level, and the weighting is of a convex function whose CAD 60 tail does the work. But its existence is what makes "any" indefensible, and a memo that has spent five drafts hunting other people's universal quantifiers does not get to print one of its own.

So the honest sentence: the dossier's mandated instrument, run on internally consistent normalised inputs, gives roughly CAD 42 against a spot of CAD 43.55. It does not condemn this stock. It declines to endorse it — by 3.2%, which is inside the noise of a dial that spans CAD 22 end to end and inside my own admitted "Low" precision on the level.

What the WAIT actually rests on, therefore, is not that gap. It is three things that do not depend on it: g₁ — a decade at 9–10% has never been printed here, and the only forward instruments in the file (new awards down 46%, backlog down 2.4%, the CIB on record as missing its disbursement targets) point the other way; the fact that the identity's own premise is falsified by the company's March 2026 equity issue; and a full-year operating margin above 4% that has never been printed on any basis in this record.

The bull case clears spot and I say so plainly. Full seasonal step, H1's actual revenue growth: bull EBIT CAD 302M, margin 4.56%, normalised EPS CAD 2.76, fair value CAD 41.41–49.69 at 15–18x. The CAD 57.00 analyst mean target is reachable on my own framework at 20.6x on that EPS — named, priced, conditioned on three things, and not adopted, since 20.6x is two and a half points above the top of my band.

The Munger Buy Zone: CAD 21 – CAD 35. Scenario-weighted normalised EPS of CAD 2.07 (bear 1.34 / central 2.02 / bull 2.76 at 25/45/30), on FY2026 revenue of CAD 6.29–6.64B, EBIT of CAD 154–311M, finance cost CAD 45M (range 36–46, both Oaktree limbs taken and sized), tax 26.5%, 68.53M shares. CAD 21 at 10x and CAD 35 as the centre of the CAD 33–38 band that g₁ of 6–8% produces — the two competing midpoint rules give CAD 34.24 and CAD 35.56, they disagree by CAD 1.32, and CAD 35 sits between them. In forward EV/EBITDA the zone is 4.7x–7.5x against a spot 9.3x — not the 16.6x the gate and I both mis-cited from a trailing base.

On the form of the trigger. I publish Moderate conviction on the verdict and Low on the level, and the gate is right that publishing "low precision" and then moving a point estimate by a dollar is in tension with itself. I have chosen the band and said so: action level CAD 35, meaning CAD 33–38, and a reader should treat any single figure inside that as the same answer. I did not choose to move it back to CAD 34 and call the precision high, and I did not widen it further to avoid committing — CAD 33–38 is what my own stated growth window produces and nothing wider is honest either.

On the floor. CAD 21 is not an outlier I invented. It is what my own model produces at g₁ = 0% — the growth rate the dossier mandates as the default (CAD 21.63 at zero growth against CAD 20.75 at 10x). I had been labelling the mandated case "absurdly cheap — back up the truck," and it cannot be absurd and mandated at once. The correct label: CAD 21 is the mandated zero-growth case, and it is where you would be paid to own the business even if the cycle never arrives — 1.51x book, 4.7x forward EV/EBITDA, roughly CAD 810M of market cap ex-cash against a CAD 10.5B order book. The action level is the ceiling, CAD 35, not the floor. Nobody has to wait for CAD 21.

I did not raise the ceiling for hidden value — the CAD 224M residual might be durable and I will not capitalise what I cannot compose. I did not raise the floor for moat quality beyond what MODERATE already bought. I note that the framework's two floor rules conflict here; my Anchor-2 sweep does not break the tie, because it is one dial, and I say so rather than pretending otherwise.

Trigger Price: CAD 21–35

Position Size: 0% at CAD 43.55. 2–3% at or below CAD 35; 5% at or below CAD 21.

Why is it mispriced? Complexity and recency. Two disclosed debt figures that reconcile only under an assumption; a receivable that broke its band with no billed/unbilled split; CAD 224M of operating cash flow whose composition is undisclosed; a seasonal business priced off trailing figures that blend its weak half with its strong one; and a filed reconciliation gap of ~CAD 23M/quarter that neither I nor the red team has resolved in four attempts. Add a +215% run in two and a half years and a nuclear narrative, and you get 21.0x normalised earnings — nine-point-eight percent compounding for a decade. Not fear, not boredom: a business genuinely hard to see through, priced by people who have stopped trying.

Moat Half-Life. Contractual: 30 years by the terms. Nuclear qualification and the national-security barrier: 10–15 years while the Ontario refurbishment and SMR cycle runs. Economic — returns durably above the 10–12% hurdle: not demonstrated, one clear year in four, and that year produced negative owner earnings.

Why not TOO UNCERTAIN. Zero tripwires fire. The business variables are now bounded by filed prints at both ends (−1.43% FY2024, +3.33% H2 2025); what remains open is a valuation parameter, disclosed as a single dial with its full map published, and the resolving events have dates. Buffett-in-character's "wrong shape, not wrong price" remains the strongest objection in the file and I still answer it the same way: bounded and dated is not the Too Hard pile.

On the outside pitch, unchanged for the fourth time. The VIC body is gated. There is no reasoning to weigh and I attribute no thesis to either author. Header metadata only. MINOR-P3-3, named so a reader does not find it unremarked: my central normalised EPS of CAD 2.03 is funkycold87's February-2024 FY2025 estimate to the decimal, and my weighted CAD 2.07 is within 2% of it. I draw nothing from either. I cannot see how his number was built, his FY2025 estimate missed by roughly five-fold on the actual, and neither author argued for this position at CAD 43.55.

On superinvestors: zero data. No named holder, no cost basis. No claim in either direction, and none available to make.

What resolves this. Q3 and Q4 give the H2 consolidated operating margin, and the annual report gives the cash-flow composition. If FY2026 prints 4.5%+ on the full seasonal step, fair value is CAD 41–50 and I was wrong to wait. If it prints near 2.7%, fair value is CAD 20–24 and today's buyer has lost 45%. The cost of waiting two quarters is a 1.76% dividend I do not need. The cost of being early is roughly CAD 15/share. That is a 30% chance I miss a move to CAD 41–50 against a 25% chance of a 45% loss.

And the last thing a reader should take from five drafts: the gate found a thumb on my scale in every one of them — eight small ones, then one large one, then one word — and the thumb was pointing the same way every time. That is information about the author, not just the memo, and it is why the level is published as a band and the conviction on it as Low. The direction I will defend: nine of twelve experts published a trigger below spot, the dossier's own mandated instrument declines to endorse the price, and a decade at 9–10% has never been printed here. The level is CAD 33–38 and anyone who tells you it is CAD 34.23 is reading a decimal off a dial that has none.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY (distilled from synthesis above)

Decision: WAIT — and it survives by 3.2%, not by the 6% I claimed last round. Trigger: Buy anywhere ≤ CAD 35 — properly, CAD 33–38, the band that g₁ of 6–8% produces; the two competing midpoint rules give CAD 34.24 and CAD 35.56 and I publish the centre rather than either. Mandated zero-growth floor CAD 21 at 10x. On scenario-weighted normalised EPS of CAD 2.07. The band is one variable: g₁ from −0.6% to 8%, against spot at 9.8%. Conviction: Moderate on the verdict — nine of twelve experts published a sub-spot trigger, a decade at 9–10% has never been printed here, and the dossier's own mandated instrument declines to endorse the price (CAD 42.15 against CAD 43.55) rather than condemning it. Low on the level, which is a choice of g₁ inside a defensible span, published as a band for that reason. Council Vote: 1 BUY, 3 HOLD, 1 WAIT, 6 PASS, 1 SELL. Nine unconditional sub-spot triggers (CAD 25.50–37); three at-spot conditional on the Q3/Q4 prints (Futurist, Historian, Lynch). Thesis in One Sentence: At CAD 43.55 the market pays 21.0x normalised earnings — 9.8% compounding for a decade at the mandated hurdle — for a narrow, cyclical contractor whose two-year outlook management guides to double-digit growth but whose forward instruments (backlog −2.4%, new awards −46%) will not carry a decade; the recovery is real, the bull case genuinely clears today's price, and the file's own mandated instrument lands within 3% of it — so this is a thin WAIT on the level and a firm one on the direction.

Load-Bearing Factors (ranked)

  1. g₁ — the decade earnings growth rate, i.e. the multiple — the entire band is one sweep of this dial: 0% → CAD 21.63, 7% → CAD 35.56, 9.4% → CAD 42.15, 9.8% → spot. The dossier mandates 0% on its stated inputs and its own formula gives 9.4% on consistent normalised ones; I sit at 6–8% and say why. Top risk: 4% growth is worth CAD 28.68 — a CAD 14.87/share, 34% permanent loss — and the mandated identity, run on my bear EPS, independently returns 4.10%.
  2. FY2026 consolidated operating margin — 2.44% to 4.68% — built from filed gross-profit prints, with the overhead level published as a range after its "fixed" premise was falsified by its own quarterly split (Q1 CAD 66.4M vs Q2 CAD 118.3M). Central 3.49–3.97%. A full-year margin above 4% has never been printed here on any basis.
  3. Composition of the CAD 224M cash-flow residual (TTM OCF 368 less normalised NI 18, D&A 106, SBC 20 — same-period basis). Durable, and TTM FCF of CAD 304M is worth CAD 37–44 at the mandated hurdle; reversing, and owner earnings give CAD 25.5–30.6. A CAD 14/share swing on one undisclosed line.

Primary Disagreement

Lynch (BUY, 62%) says the trailing multiple prices a company that no longer exists. Historian (WAIT, 55%) says receivables outrunning revenue is the tell that preceded every fixed-price-contractor casualty. The disagreement is about facts, and both are partly right in ways neither established: Lynch's CAD 57.3M residual is arithmetically impossible (H2 2025 operating profit alone was CAD 102.1M), yet his conclusion survives on the seasonality he never computed — and the receivables signal is real but its point estimate is contaminated by two unsized acquisitions and a mismatched window.

Evidence That Would Resolve This

  • FY2026 full-year consolidated operating margin — 4.5%+ makes today's price cheap; ~2.7% makes it a 45% mistake.
  • Q3/Q4 2026 new awards — the flow, not the backlog stock. This is the direct test of g₁: awards at CAD 2.0B+/quarter would begin to underwrite 9–10%; a second and third print near CAD 1.3B would confirm the CAD 1.7B addition was recognition timing, not demand.
  • Composition of operating cash flow: JV distributions versus contract-liability timing, plus a billed/unbilled and allowance split on the CAD 0.96B receivable.
  • The consolidated gross-profit-to-operating-profit bridge by quarter, which would settle whether the ~CAD 23M/quarter corporate and elimination line is fixed or variable — the premise this margin model rests on, and the one no pass has resolved.

ARE.TO — Peter Lynch Analysis

Basis used: TTM figures where noted, but per the dossier's own instruction I am NOT smuggling TTM in as steady-state — Aecon's five-year margin range is -1.4% to 3.5% and this is a cyclical, lumpy-recognition business. Where I cite forward numbers I flag them as consensus/CALC, not fact. Local cost of equity: 10–12% [dossier CALC, Beta 1.235]. Every yield below is measured against that, not a Treasury.

1. THE TEN-BAGGER TEST

Let's be honest about the category first, because false optimism is as much a Lynch sin as false pessimism. Aecon is not a fast grower — a CAD 2.98B market cap heavy-civil contractor with 0.3%–3.5% historical net margins does not become a ten-bagger. This is a turnaround sitting inside a stalwart's end market (regulated Canadian infrastructure spend). The realistic best case is a re-rating, not a multiple-expansion moonshot: analyst mean target CAD 57.00 [MEDIA, Yahoo Finance] is ~31% above the current CAD 43.55 — a good return, not a ten-bagger, and I will not pretend otherwise.

What growth is the market pricing? At TTM EV/EBITDA of 16.6x [CALC] on a depressed TTM EBITDA base (CAD 189M, which per the math above blends in a weak ~CAD 57M half-year), the market is still anchored to the old, loss-prone Aecon. What's actually achievable, if H1 2026's CAD 131.7M Adjusted EBITDA run-rate simply holds flat for H2 (no further growth assumed): full-year 2026 Adjusted EBITDA near CAD 260–265M, putting EV/forward-EBITDA closer to 11.9x — a full 4.7 turns cheaper than the trailing multiple everyone is quoting. That gap between trailing and run-rate is the whole Lynch thesis here: the market is a pessimist because it's using stale numbers.

2. THE "EVERYONE HATES IT" SIGNAL

Bearish narratives in the dossier, and the counter-argument to each:

  • "Trailing EPS is CAD -0.95, P/E not meaningful, ROE -7.0%." [yfinance/CALC] — The GAAP loss is not an operating story. Q2 2026 alone lost CAD 108.076M / CAD (1.58) per share [SEC], entirely traceable to a CAD ~128M non-cash fair-value charge booked when Aecon agreed to buy out Oaktree's preferred shares at the agreed CAD 320M purchase price [SEARCH, dated 25-Jun-2026]. Strip that one dated, sourced, non-operating item and the same quarter shows adjusted diluted EPS of CAD 0.33 vs CAD (0.10) a year ago [SEC] — a swing from loss to profit. The bears are pricing a mark-to-agreement accounting entry as if it were a business failure.
  • "Backlog is down -2.4% YoY and -46% in new awards." [SEC] — True, on the reported figure. But management states outright that the reported CAD 10,492M figure excludes a CAD 1.7B majority-share award and a separate 33.3%-share contract, both added in Q3 2026 [SEC]. Add those back and the backlog trend inverts. I'd rather have a company that under-reports backlog than one whose bankers inflate it.
  • "Insiders are selling — Rosenfeld sold 125,000 shares at CAD 41.64, Beck sold 20,000 at CAD 49.77." [SEC/SEARCH] — Look at the dates and the counter-signal. Rosenfeld's sale landed the same day the CAD 39.25 bought-deal offering closed — that reads like a director diversifying alongside a financing event, not a vote against the business. More telling: after the stock ran to CAD 49–55 in May, insiders sold there; the stock has since given back ~21% to CAD 43.55, and on 31-Jul-2026 director Jeffrey Lyash bought 3,184 shares at ~CAD 43 [SEARCH] — buying into the pullback, not chasing the top. Senior officer Terkalas and the CEO both filed planned purchases back in March at CAD 40.58 [SEC]. Insiders are trading the range, not fleeing it.
  • "Coastal GasLink settlement, Finch West cost overrun (CAD 3.7B vs CAD 2.5B estimate)." [SEARCH] — Real, and I won't wave it away. But both are legacy fixed-price contracts, and management's own framing — "the substantial completion of legacy projects including two modern LRT systems" [SEC, CEO] — plus the explicit shift toward "collaborative and progressive" contract models [SEC] is a company visibly re-engineering its risk exposure, not repeating the mistake.
  • What would a strategic buyer pay? The dossier's most underrated data point: in 2018, China Communications Construction Company tried to buy Aecon outright for CAD 1.51B, and Ottawa blocked it on national-security grounds [SEARCH]. A state-linked strategic acquirer valued this business enough to trigger a national-security review — at a valuation half of today's CAD 2.98B market cap, in a company that has since added the Gordie Howe Bridge and Eglinton Crosstown 30-year O&M positions to its concession book. Strategic scarcity value here is not hypothetical; it's been tested.

3. THE HIDDEN GROWTH

The headline "flat-to-single-digit revenue growth, 3-5% margins" story is burying a nuclear buildout growing far faster than that. In Q1 2026, Construction segment revenue grew CAD 197M YoY (+19%), and the single largest driver was nuclear operations, +CAD 104M — more than half the segment's entire growth — from refurbishment, new-build, and engineering services in Ontario and the US [SEC]. Aecon Kiewit Nuclear Partners sits inside the Darlington New Nuclear Project (BWRX-300 SMR) alliance, and site preparation was completed on time and on budget [SEARCH] — the positive half of the "both directions" project record the dossier insists I weigh against Finch West.

Is the company investing through the cycle? Yes, in a way that reads badly on a single quarter's income statement but compounds: the CAD 320M Oaktree preferred buyout retires an expensive, dilutive financing overhang (originally CAD 150M in, agreed out at CAD 320M — a 2.1x return to Oaktree that Aecon is now paying to remove from its capital structure) [SEARCH/CALC]. That's the market punishing a balance-sheet cleanup as if it were a loss.

The optionality the market is assigning roughly zero value to: 25–33% equity stakes with 30-year O&M terms in two marquee P3 assets (Eglinton Crosstown, Gordie Howe Bridge) [SEARCH], which sit inside a Concessions segment currently producing almost no revenue (CAD 2M/quarter) [SEC] simply because the annuity-like O&M cash flows haven't ramped yet. That's a call option on a long-duration cash stream trading for free inside a construction-segment multiple.

4. THE FCF REALITY CHECK

Hurdle discipline: I am using the dossier's local cost of equity, 10–12% [CALC, Canada risk-free ~3.2-3.5% + 5% ERP + levered-contractor premium], not a Treasury yield.

TTM FCF is CAD 304M [yfinance] against a CAD 2.98B market cap [yfinance] → FCF yield 10.2% [CALC]. Against a 10–12% hurdle, that sits at the bottom of the range — not screaming cheap on the trailing number alone, and I want to be honest that this is the weakest leg of my case on its own. But the owner-earnings-based Owner Yield printed in the dossier is 7.1% [CALC] — below hurdle — which is the more conservative read and the one a skeptic would use.

The reason I still lean bullish on this test: TTM FCF is calculated over a period that, per my EBITDA math above, contains roughly two quarters of much weaker 2025 performance blended with two quarters of a visibly improved 2026. If the H1 2026 Adjusted EBITDA run-rate (CAD 131.7M per half, more than triple H1 2025's CAD 38.6M) [SEC] converts to cash at anything like the historical ratio, forward FCF yield clears the top of the hurdle range, not the bottom.

"If I bought the whole company today" test: EV CAD 3.13B [yfinance], or CAD 3.45B pro forma for the CAD 320M Oaktree closing [CALC] — against a business with CAD 10.5B of contracted backlog (1.76x revenue) [SEC/CALC], two 30-year concession interests, and a nuclear-construction franchise growing double digits. At the pro-forma EV, that's roughly 13.2x run-rate EBITDA (CAD 3.45B / ~CAD 262M) — full price for the trailing record, fair-to-cheap for the trajectory.

5. THE MANAGEMENT DECODER

The Oaktree preferred buyback is the single most misread capital-allocation decision in this dossier if you only look at the GAAP loss it produced. Read as a sequence: raise CAD 172.5M of equity at CAD 39.25 (dilutive, 6.4% share-count increase) [SEARCH], then agree to pay CAD 320M to retire a CAD 150M preferred position [SEARCH]. On its face that looks like buying high (equity) to pay off an expensive obligation (preferred) — capital destruction. But the preferred was convertible and its terms aren't disclosed in this record [dossier flags this explicitly as a blind spot], meaning the alternative to paying CAD 320M cash now may have been ongoing dilution or a ratcheting conversion feature indefinitely. Removing a growing, uncapped claim on the equity with a fixed cash payment — funded partly with equity priced near the stock's current level, not at a distressed discount — is a rational simplification of the cap structure, even if the entry price with hindsight looks steep. I flag, per the dossier's own caveat, that I cannot prove this was the better of two options because the preferred's contractual terms are not in the record. But "management raised equity and paid down an expensive instrument in the same six months" is not on its face irrational, and dividend continuity (raised to CAD 0.1925/quarter) [SEARCH] alongside this suggests confidence in underlying cash generation, not distress.

No CEO departure is in this record — Servranckx is filing planned insider purchases [SEC], which is the opposite signal from a management team heading for the exits.

6. THE "WHAT IF THEY'RE RIGHT?" SCENARIO

If management's read is correct — that 2025 was the year the legacy fixed-price loss-makers (Coastal GasLink, Finch West, Eglinton, Gordie Howe) rolled off, and 2026 is the first clean year of a nuclear-and-Ontario-infrastructure-led growth phase — the business in three years looks like: Adjusted EBITDA margin sustained near or above the Q2 2026 print (5.1–5.5% consolidated, 9.5% at the Construction-segment gross-profit line) [SEC] on a revenue base growing with the CAD 37B/year Ontario capital program [SEARCH] and the nuclear buildout; EV/EBITDA re-rating from today's stale 16.6x TTM toward something closer to the 11–13x range the run-rate already implies; and a cleaner balance sheet once the CAD 320M Oaktree payment closes in Q4 2026 and stops generating quarterly noise.

BULL CASE — PRICE TARGET, TIMELINE, WHAT HAS TO GO RIGHT

Price target: CAD 57 (analyst mean, [MEDIA]) is a reasonable 12-month anchor — it implies acceptance of roughly the run-rate EBITDA math above at a still-modest low-teens multiple, not heroic multiple expansion. Timeline: 12–24 months, tracking whether Q3/Q4 2026 confirm the H1 2026 margin step-up and whether the excluded Q3 backlog additions show up in the next reported figure. What has to go right: (1) the CAD 128M Oaktree charge stays a one-off, not the first of a series of preferred/JV markdowns; (2) the AR spike (+92% vs +29% revenue) resolves as timing on new work, not collection deterioration — this is the single fact most capable of breaking the thesis; (3) no new fixed-price legacy-project write-down surfaces among the "three remaining large fixed-price legacy projects" the dossier flags as still outstanding; (4) backlog resumes growth once Q3 additions post.

Where I'd be wrong: if the AR growth is collection risk rather than timing, or if the Q2 2026 margin jump proves to be one strong quarter in a business whose five-year range (-1.4% to 3.5% net margin) is the true steady state, this is a value trap at 3.1x book, not a turnaround. I'm sizing at 3-4%, not full conviction, precisely because the dossier itself can't resolve that question and neither can I from this data alone.

---SUMMARY--- VERDICT: PASS CONFIDENCE: 84 KEY METRIC: 7.1% TTM owner yield [CALC], below the 10–12% local cost-of-equity hurdle TRIGGER PRICE: CAD 25.50–30.60 @ 12–10% required return, assuming CAD 0.21B TTM owner earnings hold [CALC] POSITION SIZE: ZERO KEY RISK: Thin margins, unresolved fixed-price claims, and receivables growing 92% against 29% revenue growth could turn apparent cash generation into another execution reversal. BULL CASE: Nuclear growth, collaborative contracting, and retirement of legacy projects could sustain Q2’s 5.1% consolidated Adjusted EBITDA margin [SEC] and make today’s valuation defensible. The Product Soul

Aecon is not an “insanely great” product company. It is a capable infrastructure contractor participating in attractive markets, with pockets of genuine integration and expertise—but it does not control demand, pricing, procurement, or the defining technology.

The Q2 improvement looks more like mid-investment recovery than deterioration: revenue rose 25%, consolidated Adjusted EBITDA doubled to CAD 82.4M, and the consolidated Adjusted EBITDA margin reached 5.1% [SEC]. But one good quarter does not establish a new product or economic identity.

1. The “No” Test — Focus

Aecon’s product line is broad but intelligible:

  • Civil infrastructure
  • Urban transportation
  • Nuclear
  • Utilities
  • Industrial construction
  • Concessions and long-term operations

That is closer to a focused infrastructure grid than an “open box” containing 50 unrelated products. Each activity draws upon the same scarce capabilities: bonding capacity, project management, regulatory qualification, skilled labour, and access to billion-dollar procurements.

But the simplicity is partly cosmetic. Each large project is effectively a bespoke product, with different partners, risk allocations, regulators, claims, and economics. The company cannot repeat one standardized product at software-like scale.

The Concessions segment is particularly awkward. It produces only about CAD 2M of quarterly revenue [SEC], roughly 0.1% of company revenue, yet contains strategically valuable 30-year interests. It adds integration and duration, but also makes the corporate story harder to evaluate.

Focus verdict: coherent, but not elegant. Aecon has said “no” to unrelated industries, not to project-level complexity.

2. The Love Metric — Insanely Great?

There is no NPS or customer-churn evidence in the dossier. For this B2G/B2B contractor, inventing consumer-style love metrics would be nonsense.

The appropriate substitute is whether clients repeatedly entrust Aecon with critical projects and whether those projects work as promised.

Evidence for customer trust:

  • Backlog was CAD 10.492B at June 30, 2026, or 1.76 times TTM revenue [SEC/CALC].
  • Aecon holds long-term roles on Eglinton Crosstown and the Gordie Howe International Bridge, including 30-year maintenance or O&M participation [SEARCH, Aecon and Crosslinx releases/Aecon IR].
  • Darlington SMR site preparation was completed on time and on budget [SEARCH, AtkinsRéalis].
  • Nuclear revenue supplied the largest Q1 2026 increase, rising CAD 104M [SEC].

Evidence against “love”:

  • Reported backlog declined 2.4% year over year and CAD 408M quarter over quarter [SEC/CALC].
  • Q2 new awards fell 46% year over year to CAD 1.269B [SEC].
  • Finch West’s total cost reached CAD 3.7B versus an initial CAD 2.5B estimate [SEARCH, “New $3.7B Finch West LRT officially open”]. The dossier cannot determine how much Aecon bore.
  • Coastal GasLink required a global settlement, while claims remained on three other large fixed-price legacy projects [SEARCH, Aecon release].
  • FY2024 produced an operating loss of CAD 60.1M [SEC].

These clients are neither fans nor trapped subscribers. They are sophisticated public agencies and utilities that rebid work, assemble consortia, and deliberately transfer execution risk. Aecon must continually re-earn its place.

Love verdict: clients trust Aecon with consequential work, but the dossier does not demonstrate preference strong enough to create pricing power or reliable retention.

3. Integration — Landlord or Tenant?

Aecon has meaningful vertical integration:

Development → financing → construction → operations and maintenance

Its concession interests can extend the relationship for 30 years. Its nuclear position also requires regulatory qualification and alliance participation that cannot be reproduced overnight. Scale, bonding capacity, and 12,000-plus employees help Aecon compete for CAD 1B-plus projects [SEARCH].

But Aecon does not own the whole widget in the Apple sense:

  • Governments and utilities control procurement and capital budgets.
  • Aecon frequently works through joint ventures and alliances.
  • It owns only 25–33% interests in cited concessions [SEARCH].
  • AtkinsRéalis is architect-engineer on the Darlington alliance, GE Vernova Hitachi supplies reactor technology, and Aecon Kiewit Nuclear Partners performs construction [SEARCH, AtkinsRéalis].
  • Collaborative procurement may improve contractual risk, but it also spreads control among more parties.

Aecon is therefore neither pure landlord nor pure tenant. It owns parts of the building and sometimes operates it for decades, but the public client owns the land, chooses the design, controls the procurement, and can invite competitors back into the lobby.

Integration verdict: strategically valuable partial integration, without ecosystem control.

4. The Next Big Thing — Creating the Future?

Aecon is positioned around powerful future-facing markets:

  • Nuclear refurbishment and new-build work
  • The Darlington BWRX-300 SMR
  • Grid and utility investment
  • Ontario’s CAD 37B projected 2026–27 capital program [SEARCH, Dentons]
  • Progressive and collaborative contracts that rebalance construction risk

This is better than optimizing a dying market. Nuclear and electrification may provide years of demand.

But Aecon is enabling that future, not inventing it. The reactor designer, utility, regulator, and government determine whether the market exists. Aecon supplies execution capacity after those decisions are made. It records CAD 0.00B of R&D in the forensic series [CALC], consistent with a builder rather than a technology originator.

Collaborative contracting is operationally important, but it is a better risk-sharing format—not a new market. Digital twins, modular construction, cloud bidding, AI, and robotics are also available to competitors. They may improve Aecon, but they do not belong to Aecon.

Creation verdict: riding the next infrastructure cycle, not creating it.

Is This Mid-Investment or Deteriorating?

The evidence supports mid-investment recovery with unresolved quality risk.

The operating evidence is positive:

  • Q2 revenue increased 25% to CAD 1.631B [SEC].
  • Consolidated operating profit reached CAD 36.4M [SEC].
  • Consolidated Adjusted EBITDA reached CAD 82.4M at a 5.1% margin [SEC].
  • H1 operating profit improved to CAD 70.5M from a CAD 15.0M loss [SEC].
  • Q1 Construction operating profit improved by CAD 41.9M year over year [SEC].
  • Legacy LRT projects reached substantial completion [SEC/SEARCH].

The balance-sheet and order-flow evidence remains uncomfortable:

  • Accounts receivable rose 92%, from CAD 0.50B to CAD 0.96B, while revenue rose 29% [CALC].
  • The billed/unbilled split and doubtful-account allowance were unavailable.
  • DPO extended from 69 days in 2023 to 83 days in 2025 [SEC].
  • Backlog and new awards declined on the reported figures [SEC/CALC], notwithstanding named Q3 additions.
  • CAD 320M remains committed for the Oaktree preferred-share purchase [SEARCH, Aecon announcement dated June 25, 2026].

The Q2 CAD 128M fair-value adjustment appears dated, non-cash, and finance-cost-related—not evidence that construction operations deteriorated [SEC/SEARCH]. But the Oaktree sequence still represents a real capital-allocation issue: CAD 150M net was raised from Oaktree in 2023, while Aecon subsequently agreed to pay CAD 320M to repurchase the position [SEARCH]. The missing contractual terms prevent a fair judgment about why.

Valuation and Hurdle Discipline

I use three different earnings views and label them explicitly:

  1. TTM owner earnings — recovery/mid-cycle basis:
    Owner earnings are CAD 0.21B [CALC], giving a 7.1% owner yield [CALC]. That is inadequate against Aecon’s 10–12% local cost of equity, derived in the dossier from Canada’s roughly 3.2–3.5% ten-year risk-free rate, an approximately 5% equity-risk premium, and a contractor-specific premium [CALC]. The trigger price of CAD 25.50–30.60 is the price at which those same owner earnings would yield 12–10% [CALC].

  2. TTM FCF — potentially elevated working-capital basis:
    CAD 304M of TTM FCF implies a 10.2% yield on the CAD 2.98B market capitalization [CALC]. That only meets the bottom of the 10–12% hurdle, and it deserves a discount because receivables expanded 92% while revenue expanded 29% [CALC]. I would not capitalize this single TTM cash-flow figure as durable mid-cycle economics.

  3. Forward earnings — optimistic mid-cycle proxy:
    Consensus EPS of CAD 2.12 implies a 20.5x forward P/E and a 4.9% earnings yield [CALC/MEDIA]. The 4.9% yield is far below the 10–12% local cost-of-equity hurdle. A no-growth valuation that merely clears that hurdle would be approximately 8.3–10.0x earnings, or CAD 17.70–21.20 per share [CALC]. Growth can justify more, but the reported backlog and award trends do not presently justify a premium of this size.

The CAD 3.13B reported enterprise value produces a 16.6x TTM EV/EBITDA multiple [CALC]. Including the committed CAD 320M purchase moves pro-forma EV closer to CAD 3.45B [CALC]. On CAD 189M of TTM EBITDA [CALC], that is approximately 18.3x pro-forma EV/EBITDA [CALC]. For a low-single-digit-margin contractor with volatile execution, this is not a forgiving valuation.

The DCF anchors of CAD 77.05 and CAD 85.43 assume 15% growth [CALC]. They do not support the current price without first proving that growth. With a 10–12% cost of equity, perpetual or near-perpetual 15% growth is economically incoherent as a terminal assumption. The negative CAD 4.64 Graham floor [CALC] shows how dependent the optimistic outputs are on growth.

What Would Change My Verdict?

I would reconsider at either:

  • CAD 25.50–30.60, where current TTM owner earnings would provide a 12–10% yield [CALC]; or
  • A higher price only after four consecutive quarters near a 5% consolidated Adjusted EBITDA margin, renewed backlog growth including the Q3 awards, normalized receivables, and resolution of the remaining legacy fixed-price claims.

I would also need evidence that the CAD 304M TTM FCF [CALC] is recurring rather than working-capital timing.

Soul Verdict: Sugared Water

Aecon is building important things, but importance is not the same as product soul.

It does not own the defining technology, control the customer experience, create the procurement market, or demonstrate measurable customer love. Its integration and nuclear qualifications give it a credible seat at the table, yet that seat must be re-won project by project.

At CAD 43.55, investors are being asked to pay for a cleaner, more durable company than Aecon has yet proven itself to be. The infrastructure is real. The transformation remains a promise.

---SUMMARY--- VERDICT: PASS CONFIDENCE: 82 KEY METRIC: 7.1% owner-earnings yield versus a 10–12% local cost-of-equity hurdle [CALC] TRIGGER PRICE: CAD 25.50–30.60 @ 10–12% required return, based on TTM owner earnings of CAD 0.21B POSITION SIZE: ZERO KEY RISK: Thin project margins, legacy fixed-price exposure, and receivables growing 92% against 29% revenue growth can turn apparent cash generation into future write-downs. BULL CASE: Four consecutive quarters near Q2’s 5.1% Adjusted EBITDA margin, replenished backlog, and successful nuclear execution under collaborative contracts would establish a much better business. MOAT FLAG: MODERATE ---END SUMMARY---

Verdict

Aecon is not an inevitable fortress, and it is not yet priced like a cigar butt. It is a capable, strategically positioned cyclical contractor whose strongest niches—nuclear qualifications, bonding capacity, and long-term concession interests—provide competitive advantages without granting broad pricing power.

At CAD 43.55, I would classify it as a mediocre-to-improving company at an insufficiently cheap price. The current 7.1% owner yield [CALC] does not meet the dossier’s 10–12% local cost of equity. The 10.2% TTM FCF yield [CALC] barely reaches the bottom of that hurdle range, but its reliability is weakened by unusually favorable-looking working-capital movements and the coming CAD 320M cash commitment.

The moat is real in selected activities, especially nuclear construction, but it does not extend to the consolidated economics. Aecon must repeatedly bid, execute, and avoid losses. That is not the business profile of an “inevitable.”

1. The Anti-Fragile Moat

The Chaos Test

Aecon is not genuinely anti-fragile.

A recession can eventually support infrastructure spending as governments use capital programs countercyclically. Ontario’s 2026 budget projects CAD 37B of capital spending for 2026–27 [SEARCH, Dentons], and the backlog equals 1.76 times TTM revenue [CALC]. Those features provide demand visibility.

But chaos also produces:

  • Government procurement delays and budget slippage.
  • Input-cost and labour volatility.
  • Credit and bonding pressure.
  • Fixed-price losses when assumptions fail.
  • Slower collections and disputed claims.

Canada Infrastructure Bank is not expected to meet its sector disbursement goals by 2027–28 [SEARCH, “Update on the Spending Outlook of the Canada Infrastructure Bank”]. Demand announcements therefore cannot be treated as equivalent to timely, profitable work.

Competitor failure could free skilled labour and reduce bidding pressure, but project failures can simultaneously damage Aecon’s own capital. Coastal GasLink required a global settlement, while three other large fixed-price legacy projects still had outstanding claims [SEARCH, Aecon release]. This is a business that may gain share during turmoil, but it can also inherit the turmoil through its contracts.

Chaos verdict: resilient demand, fragile project economics.

The CAD 1B Competitor Test

A smart rival could not reproduce Aecon’s entire position immediately with CAD 1B.

The difficult assets to recreate are:

  • Nuclear qualifications, safety processes, and participation in the Darlington alliance.
  • Bonding capacity and credibility for billion-dollar projects.
  • A workforce exceeding 12,000 [SEARCH].
  • Reference projects and relationships with governments and regulated utilities.
  • Existing 25–33% concession interests carrying 30-year O&M terms [SEARCH].

However, Aecon is already behind several substantial private competitors—PCL, EllisDon, Ledcor, and Pomerleau in cited Canadian rankings [SEARCH]. These firms do not need a hypothetical billion dollars; they already possess scale and execution histories.

Procurement reforms, progressive design-build, alliance contracting, accessible construction software, and subcontractor participation also broaden competitive access. A rival probably could not “kill” Aecon, but it could force Aecon to accept mediocre economics on work that must be continually re-won.

CAD 1B test: Aecon survives, but shareholders might not earn exceptional returns.

Pricing Power

Aecon does not possess Buffett-grade pricing power.

Pricing is constrained by competitive procurement rather than customer habit or a proprietary product. Fixed-price contracts can make cost inflation Aecon’s problem rather than the customer’s. The shift toward collaborative and progressive models [SEC] is valuable because it reduces asymmetric risk, but risk-sharing is not the same thing as unilateral pricing power.

The historical figures confirm the limitation:

  • Operating-margin series ranges from −2.0% TTM to 3.5% in 2023 [CALC].
  • FY2024 produced a CAD 60.1M operating loss [SEC].
  • FY2025 produced CAD 87.1M of operating profit [SEC].
  • Q2 2026 consolidated operating profit was only CAD 36.4M on CAD 1,631M revenue, a 2.2% margin [SEC/CALC].

A business that can raise its price tomorrow does not normally alternate between losses and low-single-digit margins because several contracts went wrong.

Pricing-power verdict: weak overall, somewhat better in qualified nuclear work.

2. Strategic Evolution

The Borg Check: Absorbing or Being Replaced?

AI, digital twins, robotics, and cloud bidding software are more likely to become tools inside Aecon than substitutes for Aecon.

Governments still need licensed entities, physical labour, bonding, safety accountability, procurement compliance, and someone legally responsible for delivery. AI cannot sign a performance bond or assume construction risk.

The danger is that these technologies become broadly available productivity tools. Cloud software starting at relatively low subscription prices can reduce administrative barriers for smaller competitors [SEARCH, construction-software market sources]. Digital twins and robotics can improve coordination, precision, safety, and rework performance across the industry [SEARCH]. If everyone can purchase the same capability, the technology improves the industry without necessarily widening Aecon’s moat.

Aecon’s position in the Darlington SMR alliance gives it a credible route to absorb modular construction rather than be displaced by it. Darlington site preparation was completed on time and on budget [SEARCH, AtkinsRéalis], and Q1 2026’s largest revenue increase was CAD 104M from nuclear operations [SEC]. Still, off-site modular manufacturing could transfer some value away from on-site contractors toward reactor designers and factory suppliers.

Evolution verdict: Aecon is unlikely to be replaced, but technology may commoditize portions of project execution.

Commoditizing the Complement

There is no evidence that Aecon is aggressively reducing the cost of an adjacent technology to strengthen a core monopoly. R&D is reported as CAD 0.00B in the forensic dataset [CALC], although construction-process investment may be recorded elsewhere.

Collaborative contracting and concession financing can make projects easier to initiate, indirectly expanding demand for construction. But that is ecosystem participation, not a classic “commoditize the complement” strategy.

This is an important distinction: Aecon is adapting its contract structure, not controlling an ecosystem.

3. Maintenance Versus Expansion

The consolidated weakness is not explained by a large loss-making expansion segment.

In Q2 2026:

  • Construction operating profit was CAD 58.5M [SEC].
  • Concessions operating profit was CAD 1.2M [SEC].
  • Consolidated operating profit was CAD 36.4M [SEC].
  • The approximate CAD 23M difference appears to be corporate costs or eliminations [CALC].
  • Concessions generated only CAD 2M of revenue and was profitable [SEC].

Construction revenue can be approximated at CAD 1,629M after subtracting CAD 2M of Concessions revenue, producing a Construction operating margin of approximately 3.6% [SEC/CALC]. Consolidated operating margin was approximately 2.2% [SEC/CALC].

Therefore, this is not a wonderful core temporarily funding a loss-making expansion. The construction core is recovering, while corporate overhead and inherently narrow project margins depress consolidated economics. Concessions is too small—and currently profitable—to explain the weakness.

The evidence supports operational recovery from legacy-project damage, not broad core deterioration in the latest quarter:

  • Q1 Construction operating profit improved to CAD 12.0M from a CAD 29.9M loss [SEC].
  • H1 operating profit improved to CAD 70.5M from a CAD 15.0M loss [SEC].
  • H1 Adjusted EBITDA rose to CAD 131.7M from CAD 38.6M [SEC].
  • Q2 Adjusted EBITDA margin reached 5.1%, versus 3.2% [SEC].

However, one or two improving quarters do not prove that the economic cycle has permanently changed. The five-year record remains too erratic.

Headline and Core P/E

Earnings basis: normalized forward/mid-cycle proxy, not peak earnings.

  • Headline trailing P/E: not meaningful because trailing EPS is CAD −0.95 [yfinance].
  • Normalized core proxy: consensus forward EPS of CAD 2.12 implies 20.5 times earnings [CALC on MEDIA consensus].
  • Corresponding earnings yield: 4.9% [CALC].
  • Required hurdle: 10–12%, the dossier’s local cost of equity.

A 4.9% prospective earnings yield is substantially below the required 10–12%. Even if consensus is achieved, the share price embeds more durability than Aecon’s historical ROIC and execution record presently justify.

Annualizing Q2 adjusted diluted EPS of CAD 0.33 would give CAD 1.32 and a P/E of approximately 33 times [SEC/CALC]. That is not a reliable mid-cycle estimate because construction earnings are seasonal and H1 adjusted basic EPS was only CAD 0.16 [SEC]. I therefore use the more favorable 20.5-times consensus figure as the normalized proxy; it still fails the hurdle.

4. Capital Allocation Machine

The Cannibal

Aecon is not a cannibal. It was a material net issuer in 2026.

The company issued 4,395,300 shares at CAD 39.25 for gross proceeds of CAD 172.5M on 27-Mar-2026 [SEARCH, Aecon offering announcement]. That represents approximately 6.4% dilution relative to the 2025 year-end share count [CALC].

It then agreed to pay CAD 320M to reacquire Oaktree’s Aecon Utilities preferred shares [SEARCH, Aecon announcement dated 25-Jun-2026]. Oaktree had invested CAD 150M net in 2023 [SEARCH]. The contractual preferred-share terms are missing, so it would be improper to declare the CAD 320M price avoidable or abusive. Nevertheless, the visible sequence—issuing common equity at CAD 39.25 and committing nearly twice the gross proceeds to redeem preferred capital—does not demonstrate shareholder-friendly cannibalism.

Raising the dividend during the same period adds another claim on capital. The trailing payout ratio is 139% [CALC], and the 1.76% dividend yield [CALC] is far below the 10–12% local cost-of-equity hurdle. The dividend is not meaningful downside protection.

ROIC Mastery

Aecon fails the required test of consistently earning more than 15% on invested capital.

Reported ROIC was:

  • 2022: 5.3% [CALC]
  • 2023: 27.3% [CALC]
  • 2024: −5.1% [CALC]
  • 2025: 5.9% [CALC]
  • TTM: −5.7% [CALC]

The TTM result is distorted by the approximately CAD 128M non-cash Aecon Utilities fair-value charge recorded in Q2 2026 [SEARCH, Q2 earnings-call transcript]. But removing that item does not transform the preceding five-year series into consistently superior economics. Only one year cleared 15%.

This is evidence of a project-based franchise with selective strengths, not a compounding capital-allocation machine.

5. Backlog and Cash Quality

The CAD 10,492M backlog equals 1.76 times TTM revenue [SEC/CALC], but the reported direction is mixed:

  • Down CAD 254M, or 2.4%, year over year [SEC/CALC].
  • Down CAD 408M from the March 2026 record [SEARCH/CALC].
  • Q2 new awards fell 46% year over year to CAD 1,269M [SEC/CALC].
  • A CAD 1.7B majority share and another 33.3% contract interest were expected to enter backlog in Q3 [SEC].

Because management says significant progressive and collaborative awards are excluded until later stages, backlog is usable as a coverage measure but unreliable as a precise leading indicator. Q3 reported additions must be observed before deciding whether recent declines represent deterioration.

Cash conversion also deserves skepticism:

  • Accounts receivable increased 92%, from CAD 0.50B to CAD 0.96B, while revenue rose 29% [CALC].
  • Accounts payable increased to CAD 1.15B [SEC].
  • DPO extended from 69 days to 83 days [SEC/CALC].
  • Working capital fell to CAD 235.7M in the latest quarter from CAD 387.9M in the preceding comparison [SEC].

The allowance for doubtful accounts and the billed-versus-unbilled receivable split were unavailable. Consequently, TTM FCF of CAD 304M and its 10.2% yield [CALC] should not be capitalized as recurring owner economics without further disclosure.

6. Valuation and Hurdle Discipline

All current-price calculations below use CAD 43.55.

Owner Earnings

TTM owner earnings are CAD 0.21B [CALC], producing the supplied owner yield of approximately 7.1% [CALC].

The hurdle is the local cost of equity of 10–12%, derived in the dossier from a Canadian risk-free rate of approximately 3.2–3.5%, a roughly 5% equity-risk premium, and an additional contractor/project-risk premium [CALC].

Thus:

  • 7.1% owner yield versus 10–12% required return: inadequate.
  • Fair market capitalization at a 10% yield: approximately CAD 2.10B [CALC].
  • Fair market capitalization at a 12% yield: approximately CAD 1.75B [CALC].
  • Using 68.53M shares, the resulting price range is approximately CAD 25.50–30.60 [CALC].

That range is my cigar-butt entry point, provided backlog and receivable quality have not deteriorated further.

Free Cash Flow

TTM FCF yield is 10.2% [CALC], compared with the same 10–12% required return.

That appears adequate only at the lowest end of the hurdle. It is not generous, particularly because:

  • Receivables grew much faster than revenue.
  • Payable days expanded.
  • Contractor cash flow is inherently lumpy.
  • CAD 320M of committed future cash outflow is not reflected in the stated CAD 3.13B enterprise value.

Using the pro-forma CAD 3.45B EV [CALC] and CAD 189M TTM EBITDA [CALC], EV/EBITDA is approximately 18.3 times [CALC], rather than the stated 16.6 times. That is demanding for a company whose operating margins and ROIC have been inconsistent.

DCF Anchors

The supplied CAD 77.05 and CAD 85.43 DCF anchors both explicitly require a 15% growth assumption [CALC]. That growth rate exceeds the 10–12% cost-of-equity range and is incompatible with a stable perpetuity formulation. The underlying horizon and terminal-value formula were not provided, so a unique independent back-solve is impossible.

I assign these anchors no weight. A contractor with a five-year record of inconsistent margins and only one year above 15% ROIC should not be valued on a 15% perpetuity-adjacent growth assumption.

7. The Yellow Pages Test

Aecon does not resemble Yellow Pages in the most important respect. Its demand is not founded on information scarcity that the internet can erase. Bridges, nuclear facilities, transit systems, and utility networks still require physical construction, regulatory approval, skilled labour, and accountable counterparties.

Its durable moat elements are:

  • Nuclear licensing and qualification requirements.
  • Bonding and balance-sheet capacity.
  • Government and utility reference relationships.
  • Existing long-term concession interests.
  • A scarce base of experienced trades and project managers.

Its temporary moat elements are:

  • Incumbency in a particular procurement pipeline.
  • Familiarity with legacy project structures.
  • Scale advantages that software and modular construction can gradually reduce.
  • Relationships that remain subject to competitive rebidding.

The decay risk is therefore not technological obsolescence. It is economic commoditization: Aecon may retain revenue and market position while returns remain poor because procurement competition transfers too much risk to the contractor.

That is subtler than a Yellow Pages collapse, but it can be equally painful for shareholders paying a premium multiple.

8. Insider and Governance Signal

The insider record is mixed but not reassuring enough to offset the valuation:

  • A director sold 125,000 shares at CAD 41.64 on the same day the CAD 39.25 equity offering closed [SEARCH/CALC].
  • Other insiders sold at CAD 49.77–55.09 during May [SEARCH].
  • Jeffrey Lyash bought in July, but sources conflict materially: either 3,184 shares or 41,796 shares [SEARCH]. The conflict cannot be resolved from the dossier.
  • Insider ownership is only 0.71% [yfinance].

These trades are not dispositive, but there is no visible pattern of substantial insider accumulation at the current price.

Total D&amp;A (Latest): CAD 106M

Accounts receivable grew from CAD 0.50B (2024) to CAD 0.96B (2025), +92%, against revenue growth of +29% (4.2B → 5.4B). That ratio is a fact, not a conclusion; it is the kind of gap Burry is seated to interrogate. No allowance-for-doubtful-accounts or unbilled-vs-billed split was retrievable — flag as missing.
