3. FATAL-F — THE MULTIPLE IS NOT DERIVED TWICE. IT IS CALIBRATED ONCE.

This is the item the task flagged as highest-risk. It is.

First, the credit the memo has earned, in full.

  • The two-stage capitalisation is arithmetically flawless. I rebuilt it: E₀ = 138.9, k = 11%, terminal 3%, n = 10.
  • g₁ = 6%: stage-1 PV 1,087.4 + terminal PV 1,127.7 = 2,215.1 (memo: 2,215) → CAD 32.32 → 15.95x ✓
  • g₁ = 8%: 1,198.4 + 1,359.6 = 2,558.0 (memo: 2,558) → CAD 37.33 → 18.42x ✓
  • g₁ = 4%: 1,920.1 → CAD 28.02 ✓ — the memo's top-risk figure, exact. Not one cell off. Credit.
  • The correction of pass 2 on 16.6x is CORRECT and I concede it. EV at spot = 68.53 × 43.55 + 174.4 = 3,158.9; forward central EBITDA = 234 + 106 = 340; 3,158.9 / 340 = 9.29x. The dossier's 16.6x is struck on TTM EBITDA of CAD 189M [refined_dossier line 187], which contains the weak window. Pass 2's ">50% compression" headline mixed a trailing spot multiple against a forward target multiple. That was period-mixing and the memo caught me at it. Withdrawn.
  • FATAL-C's data-quality substance is genuinely cured. No transaction band is asserted anywhere in Revision 3. No peer, no peer median, no "contractors change hands in that band." The dossier's PEER COMPARISON SUPPRESSED instruction is now honoured in substance as well as letter. That was the single worst violation in Revision 2 and it is gone.

Now the defect.

Anchor 2 is not a witness. It is a function. Its output is not "15.9–18.4x." Its output, on the memo's own printed table, is:

g₁ 3% 5% 6% 8% 10%
implied P/E 12.9x 14.8x 15.9x 18.4x 21.3x

Anchor 2 says 15.9–18.4x only after g₁ is fixed at 6–8%. Nothing in the two-stage model determines that window. The memo's entire stated justification is one clause: "defensible for a contractor inside an Ontario capital cycle but no faster than that, given new awards down 46%."

The anti-tautology check the memo ran tests the wrong thing. It writes: "Anchor 1 is f(moat severity count). Anchor 2 is 1/(k−g) two-staged... Neither term appears in the other; they do not reduce." That establishes the two formulas share no symbol. It does not establish that the two answers are independent. Two functions with disjoint variables are still one witness if the free parameter of the second is chosen after seeing the first. The correct test is not do the symbols reduce — it is was g₁ determined by anything other than the answer it had to produce? It was not.

And the calibration is visible across the whole range, not just at the top. Anchor 1 gives two levels: a 15–18x band and a 10x "absurdly cheap" floor. Run Anchor 2 at zero growth:

g₁ = 0%: stage-1 PV 818.0 + terminal PV 629.8 = 1,447.8 → CAD 21.13 → 10.4x

Anchor 2 at g₁ = 0% reproduces Anchor 1's 10x floor to within 4%, and the memo publishes CAD 21 as that floor. So Anchor 2 reproduces Anchor 1 at both ends once the g's are chosen. A model that can be tuned to reproduce the other anchor anywhere on its range is a calibration instrument, not a second witness.

The standing instruction that is never named. The dossier carries this, in capitals [initial_dossier lines 350–352]:

> ROE: -7.0% Retention: 0% -&gt; Sustainable growth g = ROE x retention = -0.0% > "MANDATORY: any valuation that assumes a growth rate must justify departing from this figure. Holding g fixed while varying the discount rate hides the assumption that drives the answer."

The memo departs from the mandated g by six to eight percentage points, on the input that now sets Load-Bearing Factor #1, and never mentions the instruction. It does the mirror image of what the instruction forbids: it holds k fixed at 11% and varies g, then picks a window. And the instruction's own warning is precisely diagnostic here — the assumption that drives the answer is g₁.

Here is what that single dial does. This is the whole memo:

g₁ equity value CAD/share what the memo calls it
0% (the dossier's mandated g) 1,447.8 21.13 "absurdly cheap — back up the truck"
3% 1,788 26.10 —
4% 1,920 28.02 "a 35% permanent loss, the top risk in this file"
6.9% 2,375 34.65 "fair value limit… published as CAD 35"
10.1% 2,984 43.55 spot — "not a margin of safety; that is the bet"

The published buy zone is not "10x to 16.5x." It is g₁ = 0% to g₁ = 6.9%. The floor, the ceiling, the top risk and the bet are four readings off one unsourced dial. The memo has replaced pass 2's eight small thumbs with one large one and called the consolidation a derivation.

One mitigation the memo failed to claim, and I supply it because a red team owes both directions. The 6–8% window is not invented from nothing — it appears in the council record. The Futurist's summary reads: "toward something closer to nominal GDP-plus-infrastructure-cycle growth (~6-8%)", and produces a trigger of CAD 32–37 — which brackets the memo's CAD 35 by a different route [all_summaries.md, lines 99–106]. That is a weak third witness (the Futurist's own basis is "roughly halves the pipeline's unexamined 15% assumption," arithmetic on a number the dossier says to ignore), but it is a real one and the memo does not cite it. Citing it would have been worth more than the fabricated independence claim.

FATAL-F. The sentence "Anchor 1 says 15–18x. Anchor 2 says 15.9–18.4x… Two witnesses" and the sentence "the first multiple in three drafts that I can hand a reader with a derivation attached" must not publish. What must publish instead: the multiple is a function of one chosen growth rate; here is the full map; here is why I chose 6–8% and what the dossier's mandated 0% would give.

4. THE FATAL-D IDENTITY — TRUE, TRIVIAL, AND IT CANNOT DO THE WORK ASKED OF IT

The arithmetic is right.

484.0 − 309.6 = 174.4  ✓
804.0 − 629.6 = 174.4  ✓

And it is an identity in the strict sense: (a + x) − c ≡ a − (c − x) for any x. Verified.

SERIOUS-P3-1 — The identity is true conditional on the Oaktree reading, therefore it is not evidence for it.

The second line only works if you remove the CAD 320M from the debt side at the same time you remove it from cash — i.e. only if you have already assumed the 320 inside totalDebt is the Oaktree preferred being extinguished. If the gap were leases, paying Oaktree gives 804.0 − 309.6 = 494.4, and there is no identity at all.

So the structure is: if X, both routes agree. The memo observes that they agree and writes "That is what a reconciliation looks like." It is not. It is a restatement of the assumption. Affirming the consequent — the same class of error as the fabricated reconciliation it replaces.

Scoped narrowly, because the conclusion is sound. The Oaktree reading has genuine affirmative evidence, which the memo correctly assembles: the CAD 320.0M gap matches to the decimal; the preferred was marked to CAD 320M [refined_dossier line 118]; and the CAD 128M fair-value adjustment sits in finance cost [line 120], which is how a redeemable preferred classified as a financial liability behaves — and financial liabilities are what vendors sweep into totalDebt. That case stands on its own. The "identity" adds zero and is presented as if it added everything. Strike the sentence, keep the conclusion.

SERIOUS-P3-2 — Is CAD 174.4M the right figure to value on? Yes — and it does not touch the published band, which the ledger does not admit.

Two separate problems.

(i) Net debt is inert in the published method. The buy zone is pure P/E × EPS:

CAD 21   = 10.0 × 2.10   ✓ exactly
CAD 34.65 = 16.5 × 2.10  ✓ exactly

Net debt appears nowhere. It enters only through finance cost, which the memo sets at CAD 45M from an observed run-rate, independent of which debt basis is chosen. So the executive summary's "on scenario-weighted normalised EPS of CAD 2.10 and pro-forma net debt of CAD 174.4M" is decorative: the second input does not touch the output. The memo half-admits this ("an accident of method, not a virtue") — but then books CAD +2.33 for it in the ledger. See item 6.

(ii) The memo adopts the debt reading and refuses the earnings consequence that comes with it. Under the Oaktree-is-a-financial-liability reading the memo now leads with, the preferred was carrying a return — Oaktree turned CAD 150.0M net into CAD 320M over ~32 months [refined_dossier line 210]. Retiring a CAD 320M liability accreting at anything material reduces go-forward finance cost. The memo holds finance cost at CAD 45M, i.e. flat with the pre-retirement run-rate, on the ground that "I still do not assert the preferred's terms."

That is not neutral — it is a choice, and it is bear-ward. Size it: CAD 20M of finance-cost relief = CAD 0.21 EPS = CAD 3.47/share at 16.5x. The memo adopts the balance-sheet limb of the Oaktree reading (worth +2.33 in its ledger) and declines the income-statement limb (worth up to −3.47). One reading, two limbs, only the one it can book cheaply.

5. THE BUY ZONE / BULL CASE / CAD 57 COMBINATION — INTERROGATED BOTH WAYS

All the arithmetic checks.

bull EPS   (310 − 45) × 0.735 / 68.53 = 2.842 ✓
central    (234 − 45) × 0.735 / 68.53 = 2.027 ✓  (→ E₀ = 138.9, Anchor 2&#x27;s input ✓ consistent)
weighted   0.25(1.34) + 0.45(2.03) + 0.30(2.84) = 2.1005 ✓
bull FV    15x → 42.63   18x → 51.16 ✓
CAD 57     20 × 2.84 = 56.80 ✓

Is the gap earned? Structurally, yes — and the memo is entitled to it. The buy zone applies the weighted EPS to the multiple range; the bull applies the bull EPS to the same range. Those are different questions and the memo says so. There is no Jensen problem, because a single multiple is used across scenarios: probability-weighting the values gives the same answer as weighting the EPS (I checked: at g₁ = 6%, 0.25(21.37) + 0.45(32.33) + 0.30(45.29) = 33.48, against 16.5 × 2.10 = 34.65 — consistent to within the multiple choice). The memo did not widen its band to avoid choosing; it chose CAD 35 and stated it four times.

But the interrogation cuts the other way once, and hard.

SERIOUS-P3-3 — The implied-growth headline mixes two earnings bases, and it is the most-repeated sentence in the memo.

"At CAD 43.55, on central normalised earnings, the market is paying 20.7x — which… requires 10.1% earnings growth." Both halves cannot be on the same base:

43.55 / 2.10 (weighted) = 20.74x  → interpolates to g₁ ≈ 9.7%
43.55 / 2.03 (central)  = 21.45x  → interpolates to g₁ ≈ 10.2%

The "20.7x" is on weighted earnings; the "10.1%" is on central. The memo labels both "central normalised earnings." This sentence appears in Load-Bearing Factor #1, the FINAL DECISION, the Thesis in One Sentence and the Executive Summary. The magnitude is small (9.7 vs 10.1) — but it is the exact period/basis-mixing crime for which the memo convicted itself twice and convicted pass 2 once, in the paragraph immediately adjacent. Fix the base and say which.

Same defect, smaller: "a permanent loss of CAD 15.11/share, 35% of today's price." The numerator is 43.13 − 28.02 (the 10%-growth model value less the 4%-growth model value); the denominator is 43.55 (spot). Mixed. On a single base it is either CAD 15.53 / 35.7% (spot to model) or 15.11 / 35.0% (model to model). MINOR-P3-1.

On the CAD 57: correctly handled, and I say so.

Pass 2's charge was "you withdrew the dismissal and never noticed your own arithmetic produces it." The memo now derives it (20x × 2.84 = 56.8), names the three conditions it requires, and states that 20x is two points above its defensible band. That is the correct disposition of an analyst target: named, priced, conditioned, not adopted. No finding.

On the top risk: better-founded than I expected.

I stress-tested the memo's headline risk for one-sided conditioning — does "4% growth → CAD 28.02, a 35% loss" hold central EPS fixed while stressing g? It does. But properly weighted it barely moves: at g₁ = 4% across all three earnings scenarios, 0.25(18.52) + 0.45(28.02) + 0.30(39.25) = CAD 29.02, a 33% loss. The memo's top risk survives correct conditioning. No finding. Credit.

6. SERIOUS-P3-4 — THE LEDGER IS DENOMINATED IN A METHOD THE MEMO ABANDONED

Every line's arithmetic checks:

13 × 214 − 334 = 2,448 / 68.53 = 35.72 ✓
debt 334→174:  +160 / 68.53      = +2.334 ✓ → 38.06 ✓
EBIT 214→234:  +20 × 13 / 68.53  = +3.794 ✓ → 41.85 ✓
multiple:      16.5 × 2.027       = 33.45 ✓ → −8.40 ✓
weighting:     16.5 × 2.10        = 34.65 ✓ → +1.20 ✓
gross 15.72, net −1.07 ✓

Three defects, none of them arithmetic.

(i) Two of the three bull-ward credits are paid in a currency the memo no longer accepts. Lines 2 and 3 are computed inside the 13x EV/EBIT framework that line 4 abolishes. Re-price them in the published method (P/E × EPS): - The +CAD 2.33 debt credit is worth CAD 0.00 to the published band. Net debt does not enter 16.5 × EPS; finance cost is set independently at CAD 45M. The memo says this itself twelve paragraphs earlier and then books the credit anyway. - The +CAD 3.79 earnings credit is worth +CAD 3.55, not 3.79, in the new method (16.5 × [(234−45) − (214−45)] × 0.735 / 68.53).

Honest re-statement of the response to four FATAL findings: −8.40 (multiple) + 3.55 (earnings) + 0.00 (debt) = CAD −4.85, then +1.20 self-introduced. Not "CAD 15.72 of gross movement netting to −1.07."

(ii) The +1.20 line is not responsive to anything. Scenario-weighting the EPS was not asked for by either red-team pass. It is a bull-ward methodological change the memo introduced on its own initiative, booked in a ledger whose stated purpose is to answer the charge that concessions moved no dollars. Strip it and the four FATAL-responsive lines net to CAD −2.27.

(iii) The largest line is the least sourced. The −8.40 is entirely a function of g₁ (FATAL-F). At the dossier's mandated g = 0% the multiple correction would be roughly −17; at the market's implied 10% it would be +1. The single line that dominates the ledger has a sign and a magnitude determined by an unsourced dial.

Is −1.07 defensible? The number is not the problem. A memo is entitled to a small net after offsetting corrections — that is what "offsetting" means, and the memo's willingness to make the two bull-ward corrections in full (+2.33, +3.79) and let the bear-ward one exceed them is honest work, not evasion. The problem is that the ledger is presented as an audit of the memo's own discipline while being denominated in the abandoned method, containing one credit worth zero and one line that answers no charge. It is a real improvement in form over pass 2's "concede in prose." It is not yet the audit it claims to be.

7. THE CAD 42.09 REBUTTAL — THE MEMO IS RIGHT. I CONCEDE, WITH ONE QUALIFICATION.

The memo's reply: "CAD 42.09 is computed at 13x EV/EBIT — the parameter the gate had just convicted… You cannot strike a parameter as underived and then use it to set fair value… CAD 42.09 is not a fair value. It is a measurement of how much leverage the unsourced parameter had."

That is correct, and it is a better reading of pass 2's argument than pass 2 gave itself. CAD 42.09 was constructed as a reductio — "on your own inputs, corrected only where you failed to source, your answer is 42 not 36" — and a reductio does not assert a fair value. Pass 2 then abandoned that framing and wrote "the memo's own corrected arithmetic says the stock is roughly fairly valued at CAD 42" and "CAD 42 is the ceiling." That second step promoted a reductio to a valuation while its key input stood convicted. Overreach. Withdrawn. The memo wins this exchange cleanly and the reply is not a dodge.

The qualification. Pass 2's charge had two halves and the memo answers one. The second half was: if you are going to move the multiple you must say which direction and why, because every parameter you never sourced sits at the bear end. The memo's answer — "I derived it and it came out lower" — is valid only if the derivation is independent. It is not (FATAL-F). So: logically correct, evidentially unearned. The reply disposes of pass 2's number; it does not dispose of pass 2's structural charge, which has simply relocated from eight parameters to one.

PART 2 — (a) IS THE MEMO STILL TOO GENEROUS?

Less so than either predecessor, and the surviving generosity is concentrated, not diffuse.

Beyond FATAL-E and FATAL-F:

SERIOUS-P3-5 — Three different bull weights, and only the smallest one touches a number

The memo publishes: bulls at 55% on FY2026 earnings; bulls at 35% on value at CAD 43.55; and scenario probabilities of bear 25 / central 45 / bull 30. Only the 30% is operative — it is what produces weighted EPS 2.10 and therefore the entire buy zone. The 55% and the 35% are used nowhere and change nothing. This is pass 2's "conceded in prose, moved no dollars" charge, reproduced verbatim, in the one section the ledger does not cover. A memo that announces a 55% bull weight and computes on 30% has not raised its bull weight.

SERIOUS-P3-6 — "A growth rate nothing in this file supports" is false as written

The Thesis in One Sentence and the Executive Summary both say the reader is "asked to pre-pay for a growth rate nothing in this file supports." The file contains, all retrieved and all bull-ward on growth: - Management guidance of double-digit revenue growth in 2026 and further growth in 2027 [initial_dossier line 120] — the only multi-year forward statement in the record, and the memo never cites it once; - Ontario's CAD 37B 2026–27 capital budget [refined_dossier line 262]; - A named CAD 1.7B Q3 backlog addition plus a separate 33.3%-share award [line 154]; - Darlington site prep on time and on budget [line 252]; - H1 2026 revenue +22.2% — the memo's own figure.

None of that proves 10% for a decade, and the memo's bear instruments (backlog −2.4%, new awards −46%) are real and correctly weighted. But "nothing in this file supports" is not a characterisation, it is a factual claim about the file, and it is wrong. Narrow it or drop it. (The memo made this exact error in reverse in Revision 2 and had to reinstate a narrowed sentence — see its own SERIOUS-6 concession. Same failure mode, opposite direction, one page apart.)

SERIOUS-P3-7 — The one observed valuation route is published and then discarded

The memo publishes TTM FCF of CAD 304M at the mandated hurdle → CAD 36.97–44.36, and TTM owner earnings of CAD 210M → CAD 25.54–30.64 (all four verified: 304/.10=3,040→44.36; 304/.12=2,533→36.97; 210/.10=2,100→30.64; 210/.12=1,750→25.54). It then declines to let either touch the band, on the ground that "neither is independent of the earnings route (both capitalise a stream)."

That reasoning is too strong. Both capitalise a stream, yes — but one capitalises an observed TTM cash flow and the other capitalises a forecast normalised EPS built from a margin model with a contested overhead parameter. Those are not the same witness. The memo used "not independent" to discard the only route in the document that requires no forecast. Mitigating, and I state it: the FCF route's range (25.5–44.4) substantially overlaps the published 21–35 and its midpoint sits near the CAD 35 ceiling, so the practical effect is small and mildly corroborating. But the reason given for the discard is not the real reason.

MINOR-P3-2 — Lyash is not "the most recent insider transaction in the record"

The memo says so twice. The register shows Nackan selling on 2026-08-06 and 2026-08-18, both after Lyash's 2026-07-31 purchase [refined_dossier lines 232–233]. Bull-ward as written. (Pass 2 made the identical error; I am correcting myself as well.)

MINOR-P3-3 — The memo's own central EPS is CAD 2.03, which is funkycold87's FY2025 estimate to the decimal

The memo notes that its weighted 2.10 is within 1.5% of the VIC 2.03 and draws nothing from it — correct. It does not notice that its central EPS is 2.03 exactly. Nothing is built on it and no thesis is attributed; a reader will notice, so name it and dismiss it rather than leaving it to be found.

PART 3 — (b) HAS IT OVERCORRECTED?

Short answer: NO on the floor, NO on the ceiling — but the imbalance pass 2 found has been CONSOLIDATED, not fixed.

The free-parameter inventory, re-run

Pass 2 found eight bear-ward and one bull-ward. Revision 3:

# parameter status in Revision 3 direction
1 overhead F, 2025 FIXED — 153.5/half, reproduces filed FY2025 GP exactly —
2 overhead F, 2026 184.7/half; half filed, half flagged; "fixed" falsified now bull
3 seasonal-step split FIXED — both bounds published and rolled forward consistently —
4 margin haircut 3.55→3.4% REMOVED ✓ —
5 net debt basis FIXED — both published, evidence led with; and inert —
6 receivables central FIXED — 241, refuted endpoint dropped; never deducted from value —
7 FCF hurdle FIXED — 10–12% mandated range used ✓ —
8 cycle-discounted multiple on the bull FIXED — bull now struck at 15–18x ✓ —
9 H2 2026 revenue growth both endpoints published; centre +15% labelled a judgment bear, disclosed
10 g₁ = 6–8% → the multiple UNSOURCED; mandated g never addressed the whole answer
11 finance cost 45, no credit for retiring a CAD 320M liability asserted bear, ~CAD 3.5/sh
12 tax 26.5% on consolidated EBIT incl. equity-accounted JV income unchanged, unremarked bear, small
13 scenario probabilities 25/45/30 asserted, and stated as attackable ambiguous
14 55% / 35% bull weights decorative — neither is used bear in effect

Six of pass 2's eight are genuinely, arithmetically fixed. That is real and it should be said first. But the count is the wrong metric. Pass 2's eight thumbs moved CAD 1–4 each. Revision 3 has one parameter that moves CAD 23 across the range the memo itself publishes, and it is unsourced. Concentration is not correction. The memo swapped eight thumbs for one fist and called it a derivation.

Are the scenario points thumbed? Barely — and this is important evidence.

I re-ran the weighted EPS using the midpoint of every range the memo itself publishes, instead of its chosen points:

memo's point midpoint of memo's own published range
bear EBIT 170 (→ EPS 1.34) 185.5 of 154–217 (→ 1.50)
central EBIT 234 (→ 2.03) 242 of 221–263 (→ 2.11)
bull EBIT 310 (→ 2.84) 297.5 of 285–310 (→ 2.71)
weighted EPS 2.10 2.1375
ceiling at 16.5x 34.65 35.27

The residual scenario-point thumb is CAD 0.62 — and the memo picks the top of its bull range while picking below the midpoint of bear and central, so it is not even uniformly bear-ward. Compared to Revision 2, where the equivalent exercise moved the answer by CAD 6+, this is a transformation. Pass 2's structural charge is no longer true at the scenario level. It is true at exactly one place: g₁.

Is a CAD 21 floor defensible?

Yes as a floor — but the memo has mislabelled it, and the mislabel is the most consequential thing in this section.

At CAD 21 you would be paying: - 1.51x book (BV/share 13.93) - 4.7x forward EV/EBITDA (21 × 68.53 + 174.4 = 1,613 / 340) - 0.26x sales on FY2026 revenue of ~6.3B - CAD 1,439M of market cap against CAD 629.6M of cash — roughly CAD 810M ex-cash for a business with a CAD 10.5B backlog (1.76x revenue), H1 2026 Adjusted EBITDA of CAD 131.7M vs 38.6M, and a director buying at ~CAD 43 six weeks ago.

That is a genuine back-up-the-truck level and the label is not overcorrected.

But here is what the memo does not tell the reader. I ran its own Anchor 2 at zero growth: CAD 21.13. The CAD 21 floor is not an "absurdly cheap" outlier. It is precisely what the memo's own model produces at the growth rate the dossier mandates as the default. The memo labels the dossier's base case "absurdly cheap — back up the truck," departs from it by seven points to reach its CAD 35 ceiling, and never names the instruction requiring it to justify that departure.

Read both ways, as required: - Against overcorrection: the floor is not punitive — on the memo's own instrument it is the mandated case, and the memo is being generous by publishing an action level 65% above it. - Against generosity: the CAD 35 ceiling requires a six-to-seven-point departure from the mandated g, supported by one clause. If the dossier's instruction is honoured on its own terms, the memo's fair value is CAD 21–26, not CAD 35.

The memo is not too generous or too punitive. It is under-determined, and it has published the ambiguity as a range without telling the reader that the range is one variable. That is the honest finding for section (b), and it is why FATAL-F is fatal rather than serious.

The specific facts the task asked me to weigh against the floor

CAD 629.6M cash, CAD 10.5B backlog, H1 Adjusted EBITDA CAD 131.7M vs 38.6M, and a director buying at ~spot on 2026-07-31 — all four are in the memo, all four are reported in both directions, and none is suppressed. The cash is netted for the CAD 320M commitment and the memo says so. The backlog is reinstated against the memo's own thesis (SERIOUS-1 conceded). The EBITDA swing is cited as the reason the bull weight on earnings rose. The Lyash buy is reported, sized on both conflicting feeds, and explicitly noted as sitting above the entire buy zone. That is not a memo suppressing the bull case. It is a memo whose one remaining lever happens to point down.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

I'll start with the part I got wrong, because if I don't say it first nobody has to believe the rest.

I told you last time you'd made up a hundred and eighty-five million dollars. I went and looked. It's on line two hundred sixty-eight of the raw file — Q1, gross profit, hundred and a half, right next to revenue of twelve fifty-seven, and twelve fifty-seven is the whole company because the other segment is two million dollars. You had the number and I didn't look hard enough. That's mine and I'm eating it.

Same with the sixteen-and-a-half times EBITDA. That's a trailing figure struck on the bad twelve months and I ran it against your forward number and hollered about a fifty percent haircut. On forward EBITDA you're at nine-point-three today. You caught me mixing periods in the same breath I was scolding you for mixing periods. And the forty-two dollars — you're right, that was my reductio and then I promoted it to a price. You can't strike a parameter and then trade on it. All three of those are yours.

Now. You still put your thumb somewhere, and this time you only put it in one place.

Take that hundred eighty-five million and split it by quarter. Sixty-six in the first, a hundred eighteen in the second. That's your fixed cost damn near doubling in ninety days. It isn't fixed. And if it isn't fixed, you can't hold it still across a half, which is the only thing your whole model does. Worse — run your own model backwards. If it's ninety-two a quarter, Q1 gross profit had to be a hundred twenty-six. The filed number is a hundred and a half. The very line you cite to prove the number is filed is the line that says your model's wrong. Then you gave me a decomposition — MG and A of a hundred eighteen, corporate of forty-six, residual of twenty — and the hundred eighteen is a consolidated ratio that already has the corporate in it. You counted it twice and the third piece is whatever was left over. That's not a decomposition, son, that's a plug with a haircut.

And then the multiple. This is the third time.

You told me you'd derived it twice and you ran a symbolic check to prove the two didn't reduce. They don't share a letter, that's true. But look what you actually did. Your second anchor isn't a number — it's a machine with a crank on it. Turn the crank to three percent, out comes thirteen times. Turn it to ten, out comes twenty-one. You turned it to six-to-eight and out came fifteen-point-nine to eighteen-point-four, and then you turned around and told me it agreed with the fifteen-to-eighteen you'd gotten from the moat count. Of course it agreed. You turned it until it did.

And I'll show you it's a crank and not a witness, because I turned it myself. Set the growth to zero — which is the number the dossier mandates you use unless you justify leaving it, and it says so in capital letters and you never once mention it — and your machine prints twenty-one dollars and thirteen cents. Which is your floor, which you call "absurdly cheap, back up the truck." Your floor, your ceiling, your top risk and today's price are zero, seven, four, and ten percent on the same dial. Your whole memo is one number wearing four labels.

The rest of it, I'll take. The nineteen-page ledger — every line of it adds up, though two of your credits are denominated in a method you threw away on the very next line and one of them is worth precisely nothing to the answer you published. The stress test — you threw it out instead of picking whichever version you liked, and that's the first time I've seen anyone in this building do that. The Lyash trade, sized both ways. The peer band, gone. The VIC page, still shut, and when your own number landed a penny and a half from that fellow's estimate you didn't take it. That's discipline.

Where I end up: you fixed six of my eight thumbs and put all the weight on the seventh. That's not a fix, that's a load transfer. Go tell the reader the multiple is one growth rate, print the whole map from zero to ten, and say out loud what the rulebook told you to use.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

I raised two objections and this fellow has now answered both of them properly, so let me settle up before I complain.

I said he bounded his range with one fact and one forecast. He went and rebuilt it out of the filings — and the test I care about, he passed: his reconstruction of 2025 adds back up to the gross profit the company actually printed. Three hundred ninety-four point one. When your model reproduces a filed number you didn't feed it, that's worth something, and I've seen very few people manage it. I said he'd sized the seller and not the buyer. He's sized both, on both of the conflicting reports, and he told the reader he'd previously taken the price off one and the share count off the other. I said the trigger and the verdict had come apart. They haven't, this time — he says buy at thirty-five and he means it and he says it four times.

Now, what I still can't get past.

He's built a machine that turns a growth rate into a price, and it's a fine machine — I checked his arithmetic at four different settings and he was exact every time. But the dossier handed him a growth rate, and it put the word mandatory on it, and it said if you're going to use a different one you have to say why. He used a different one. Six to eight percent. And the reason he gives is one sentence about the Ontario capital cycle. A sentence isn't a derivation, and this isn't a small input — it's the only input left. Everything else in his memo he's now bolted down.

Run his own machine at the number he was told to use and you get twenty-one dollars. He calls twenty-one dollars "absurdly cheap." Well now. It can't be absurd and mandated at the same time. One of those two words is wrong, and he needs to tell the reader which.

And there's a thing in that file he never picks up, which surprises me because he's picked up everything else. The company told the market it expects double-digit revenue growth this year and further growth in 2027. That's the only forward statement of any length in the record. He writes that the reader is being asked to pre-pay for a growth rate "nothing in this file supports." That's not so, and he made the same mistake in the other direction two drafts ago and had to walk it back. You don't have to believe management. You do have to say they said it.

On Rule Number One. At twenty-one dollars you'd own this thing for a shade over one-and-a-half times book, four-and-three-quarters times next year's cash earnings before depreciation, with six hundred thirty million of cash on the balance sheet and a ten-and-a-half billion order book. That's not a punitive floor. That's a fine floor. The trouble isn't the floor — it's that he can't tell you why the ceiling is thirty-five and not twenty-six, except that he chose a growth rate.

Would Berkshire buy it? No, and still for the shape. Ninety-nine point nine percent of the revenue gets re-bid. But I'll say this for the man: three drafts, seven passes' worth of findings, and he never once reached for the gated write-up, never once invented a smart-money holder out of an empty registry, and when his own arithmetic handed him a flattering coincidence he refused it. I'd rather read a memo with one honest hole in it than five with none admitted.

THE "OLD SCHOOL" FINAL SCORECARD

  • Circle of Competence: In. Upgraded again. The margin is now built from filed gross-profit prints with a reconstruction that reproduces FY2025's filed figure exactly. The remaining uncertainty is a valuation parameter, not a business one.
  • Moat Integrity: Narrow, cyclical, unproven above cost of capital. Correct, stable across three drafts, CCCI counted both ways, and the memo now correctly answers Buffett-in-character on where the fence gets paid for (MODERATE → 15–18x rather than 10x). Sound.
  • Management Character: Mixed, leaning Promoter. Sherlock's sequence — equity issued at a discount, dividend raised the same quarter on a 139% payout, CAD 320M committed within six months — survives a third pass untouched and remains the strongest independent finding in the council.
  • Berkshire Buy? No. For the shape, not the price.
  • Graham-Dodd Grade: C− for the security (negative Graham Floor, 3.1x book, 20.7x forward on an unproven margin, 1.76% carry, 99.9% of revenue re-bid). A− for the memo — up from B/B+ and from B−. Six of eight bear-ward parameters arithmetically eliminated; the residual scenario-point thumb is CAD 0.62; the stress test withdrawn rather than cherry-picked; the peer violation eliminated; three red-team findings correctly rebutted and one red-team error correctly identified. Held below A by exactly two things: one falsified premise and one calibration presented as corroboration.

VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

WAIT at CAD 43.55 with an action level at CAD 35 is a coherent, well-constructed verdict — and it rests on a single unsourced number that the memo presents as having been derived twice.

The published band is not "10x–16.5x on weighted normalised earnings." It is g₁ ∈ [0%, 6.9%], with spot at g₁ = 10.1%. Four labels, one dial. Until that is disclosed, a reader cannot tell whether the CAD 35 is a valuation or a preference.

REQUIRED BEFORE PUBLICATION

  1. Strike "F = 184.7 is a filed number, not an assumption." Publish the quarterly decomposition (Q1 66.4 / Q2 118.3) and state that the fixed premise is not supported by it. Answer the dossier's actual warning — corporate cost and eliminations — not the Concessions question. Withdraw the 118 + 46 + 20 decomposition: the first term already contains the second and the third is a residual. 1b. The new mitigation — verified independently, and it holds

The claim under suspicion: "The proportional treatment reduces algebraically to the raw operating-margin step and needs no F at all."

I derived it symbolically without reference to the memo's numbers. Under a proportional treatment, overhead is F = f·R. Then

OM = (GP − f·R)/R = GM − f

f is a constant across both halves, so it differs out of the seasonal step:

ΔOM = OM_H2 − OM_H1 = (GM_H2 − f) − (GM_H1 − f) = ΔGM

and the projection OP_FY = OP_H1 + (OM_H1 + φ·ΔOM)·R_H2 contains no F and no f anywhere. The claim is true, and it is true for the strongest reason available: the parameter does not cancel out of a fitted expression, it never enters. This is the opposite of a smuggled assumption — it is a reduction that removes a free parameter rather than one that hides it. CHECK 0 clears it.

Numerically, from filed prints only:

ΔOM(2025) = 102.1/3,066.3 − (−15.0/2,363.7) = 3.3298% − (−0.6346%) = 3.9643 pp
OM_H1(2026) = 70.5/2,888.0 = 2.4411%
full step, R = 6,635:  70.5 + (2.4411+3.9643)% × 3,747 = 310.6 → 4.681%   [memo 4.68 ✓]
full step, R = 6,292:  70.5 + 6.4055% × 3,404          = 288.6 → 4.587%   [memo 4.59 ✓]
no step (any R):       OM_FY = OM_H1                            = 2.4411% [memo 2.44 ✓]

Identical under F = 184.7 and F = 132.8, exactly as the memo's table asserts. No F required. Verified.

1c. Every cell of the two-F table reproduces

step F = 184.7 fixed F = 132.8 fixed proportional (either F)
no step 2.97 – 3.27 2.82 – 3.04 2.44
half step 3.64 – 3.97 3.49 – 3.74 3.51 – 3.56
full step 4.31 – 4.67 4.16 – 4.43 4.59 – 4.68

Published ceiling 4.16 – 4.68% ✓. Published central 3.49 – 3.97% ✓. The stated EPS cost of the correction — CAD 0.09 — is the bull leg (8 × 0.735 / 68.53 = 0.086) and is correct as stated.

The fixed-F seasonal step of +2.4763 pp still regenerates FY2025's filed gross profit to the decimal (138.5 + 255.6 = 394.1). Third time I have run that check; third time it passes.

1d. The midpoint scheme is genuine, symmetric, and not hand-picked — SERIOUS-P3-5's harder half, discharged

Pass 3 charged that the scenario points were chosen. Revision 4 claims they are now midpoints. I could not break the scheme. It pairs each scenario with a matching revenue endpoint and takes the min–max midpoint of the four treatment cells at that endpoint:

bear    = no-step   at R = 6,292 : range 2.44–2.97% → mid 2.703% → EBIT 170.1   [memo 170]
central = half-step at R = 6,414 : range 3.53–3.76% → mid 3.644% → EBIT 233.7   [memo 233.8]

Three scenarios, one rule, applied identically, reproducing all three published points including the ledger's 233.8 to one decimal. Pass 3's residual CAD 0.62 scenario-point thumb is now zero. My pass-3 arithmetic that predicted midpointing would give CAD 35.27 was computed against Revision 3's narrower ranges and before the low-F column existed; it is superseded, and the divergence is explained entirely by the FATAL-E correction the gate itself demanded.

2b. Verifying the normalisation inputs — derived, not chosen, and contaminated against the memo

The task asked whether the move from ROE −7.0% to +14.54% is derived or chosen. Derived. The whole construction collapses to one line:

g = ROE × retention = (E/B) × (1 − D/E) = (E − D)/B
  = (2.025 − 0.77) / 13.93 = 9.009%

Book value per share 13.93 is the dossier's own figure [initial_dossier L353; refined L107, L182]. Dividend 0.77 is the dossier's own figure [initial_dossier L347]. Shares 68.53M cancel entirely — 68.53 × 13.93 = 954.6 and 2.025 × 68.53 = 138.8 are decoration; nothing depends on the share count. So there is no chosen input in the ROE step at all beyond the earnings figure.

Is the numerator contaminated by the CAD 128M charge? No — and the denominator is, in the memo's disfavour. The numerator is the memo's forward normalised EPS from the margin model; the charge is excluded by construction. The denominator — book equity of 954.6 — has been reduced by that same charge, which inflates ROE and therefore inflates g. Since the memo deploys 9.01% as the most bull-ward reading of the mandated formula and then argues even that falls short of spot, the contamination makes its own argument harder, not easier. The memo names it ("struck on a book equity depressed by successive loss years"). Not self-serving. Credit.

And one genuine corroboration the memo did not notice. Run the same formula on the memo's bear EPS: (1.3427 − 0.77)/13.93 = 4.11% → fair value CAD 28.91. The memo's independently-constructed top-risk case is CAD 28.68 at 4%. The dossier's mandated instrument, run on the memo's bear scenario, reproduces the memo's top risk to within 0.8% — and it does so through a formula that shares no input with the margin model except the EPS itself. That is the first thing in four passes that is actually a second witness for a memo number, and it is sitting unclaimed. Cite it.

2c. FATAL-G — "no reading of the mandated formula, on any inputs, clears CAD 41" is false, and it is falsified by the memo's own headline earnings figure

The sentence, in two places:

> "So: no reading of the dossier's mandated growth formula, on any inputs, produces a fair value above CAD 41. That is what the WAIT actually rests on now." [FINAL DECISION, L169] > > "Conviction: Moderate on the verdict — no reading of the dossier's own mandated growth formula, on any inputs, values this above CAD 41.07 against a spot of CAD 43.55." [EXECUTIVE SUMMARY, L201]

The formula is fed central EPS 2.025. The value it produces is then discounted off E₀ = 142.2 — which is the weighted EPS 2.0742. The growth rate and the earnings level it grows are struck on two different earnings bases, inside a single calculation, three sections after the memo convicted itself of exactly this crime on "20.7x → 10.1%" and fixed it.

Put both on one base — the memo's own primary base, the one its valuation table already uses:

E = 2.0742 (weighted normalised EPS — the memo&#x27;s E₀)
ROE       = 2.0742 / 13.93                = 14.89%
retention = 1 − 0.77/2.0742               = 62.88%
g         = 14.89% × 62.88%               =  9.36%
fair value at g = 9.36%, E₀ = 142.2       = CAD 42.17

CAD 42.17 > CAD 41. The claim is false, and it is false on the more internally consistent of the two available inputs. Nothing exotic was needed: same book value, same dividend, same formula, same discount rate, same terminal rate, same E₀ — one substitution, of the memo's own headline number for its own subsidiary one.

Push harder, as instructed, and readings that clear spot exist. Applied per-scenario — which is what the identity literally says, since ROE and payout are state-dependent — and weighted at the memo's own 25/45/30:

bear   g = 4.11%  → CAD 28.91
central g = 9.01% → CAD 41.10
bull   g = 14.27% → CAD 60.16
weighted                      → CAD 44.25   (&gt; spot 43.55)

I do not endorse that reading and I will not pretend it flips the verdict. It stacks a bull-scenario ROE into a ten-year growth rate and a bull earnings level, and the weighting is of a convex function — the CAD 60.16 tail is doing the work. A competent analyst could publish it; a careful one would not. But its existence is dispositive against the word "any."

Scope of the damage, stated honestly and not one dollar more:

  • The verdict does NOT change. CAD 42.17 is still below spot. WAIT survives.
  • The trigger does NOT change. This calculation feeds nothing; the band is multiple × EPS.
  • The stated basis of the verdict changes materially. The memo says the mandated formula's most bull-ward reading is "still 6% below CAD 43.55." On one consistent base it is 3.2% below — inside the noise of an instrument whose single dial spans CAD 22 from end to end, and inside the memo's own admitted "Low" precision. A 3.2% gap cannot carry the weight of "that is what the WAIT actually rests on."

What must publish instead: the dossier's mandated formula, run on internally consistent normalised inputs, gives g = 9.4% and a fair value of roughly CAD 42 — approximately today's price, not comfortably below it. The mandated instrument does not condemn the stock; it declines to endorse it. The WAIT rests on the multiple, on backlog and new-award flow, and on the fact that a decade of 9–10% has never been printed here — not on a 6% gap that is really 3%.

That is a weaker sentence. It is also the true one, and the memo is measurably better at accepting those than it was three drafts ago.

SERIOUS-P4-2 — the Oaktree sizing does not close, and the error is bull-ward

The memo sizes the two limbs as relief CAD 10–20M/yr against charge CAD 11M/yr (320 × 3.5%), then writes:

> "Net: CAD −7M to +9M, central zero. Finance cost therefore stays at CAD 45M… with the range CAD 30–45M published."

10 − 11 = −1 and 20 − 11 = +9. The net is −1 to +9, not −7 to +9; the −7 end does not follow from either stated figure. And a net of −1 to +9 gives a finance-cost range of 36 to 46, not 30 to 45. The published CAD 30M floor requires CAD 15M of net relief, which is CAD 6M more than its own top-of-range sizing allows.

Consequence: the CAD 2.65/share sensitivity offered to the reader is the full 15-point span (45 − 30); on the memo's own sizing it is 9 × 0.735 / 68.53 × 16.5 = CAD 1.59. The memo is offering a reader roughly CAD 1.06/share more upside than its own arithmetic supports. Small, bull-ward, and it is in new Revision-4 text. Fix the two numbers or drop the 30 floor.

The memo took my citation at face value, which is the correct behaviour, and inherited my mistake. Mine, stated plainly, and I am correcting myself for the third consecutive pass.

Bear-ward. Correct it to CAD 0.64 or state honestly that the file contains no basis for sizing a forward impairment.

PART (a) — IS ANYTHING STILL TOO GENEROUS?

Almost nothing, and I say that having gone looking.

  • The CAD 30–45M finance-cost range and its CAD 2.65/share sensitivity (SERIOUS-P4-2) — roughly CAD 1.06/share of unsupported reader upside. The only bull-ward excess I can find in the document.
  • Minor rounding asymmetry: 10 × 2.0742 = 20.74 published as CAD 21 (up 0.26) while 16.5 × 2.0742 = 34.22 is published as CAD 34 (down 0.22). The floor rounds toward the reader, the ceiling away. Both immaterial and they roughly offset. No finding.
  • E₀ rounding drift in the Anchor-2 table: the 0% row requires E₀ = 142.2 while the 6.5% row requires ≈141.9 (I get 34.30, memo prints 34.23). ±CAD 0.07. MINOR-P4-3. Immaterial, but visible to a reader who checks, and this memo is now read by people who check.

Everything else on my pass-3 generosity list has been removed rather than argued with. That is worth stating flatly: of the seven SERIOUS items and three MINOR items I raised, nine are discharged in full and one is discharged with the arithmetic error above.

PART (b) — HAS REVISION 4 OVERCORRECTED?

On the trigger move CAD 35 → 34: NO. It is derived, not chosen — I tried to break it and could not.

The move is CAD 0.42 (34.65 → 34.23) and it is fully accounted for: weighted EPS falls 2.10 → 2.07 because the three scenario points moved to a symmetric midpoint rule (which I reproduced exactly, above), and because FATAL-E's correction — the correction the gate demanded — added a lower overhead column that pulls the bull cell down. The memo did not ratchet. The gate moved it, and it moved by the amount the gate's own finding was worth. Pass 3 predicted midpointing would give CAD 35.27; the difference between that and 34.23 is entirely the F = 132.8 column, which did not exist when I made the prediction.

The strongest available overcorrection charge, tested and rejected. The memo adopted the downward alternative overhead build (F = 132.8, from Q1's implied run-rate) and did not construct its mirror (F = 236.6, from Q2's). Holding Q2's overhead constant would give a bull ceiling of 4.90%, bull EBIT ≈ 310, and would cancel the entire Revision 4 downgrade. On its face that is one-sided.

It does not survive inspection, and the memo is right. A genuinely fixed cost cannot exceed the smallest quarter in which it was incurred — Q1 demonstrably absorbed only CAD 66.4M, so F_fixed ≤ 132.8/half is a bound, not a preference, and the remainder is by definition variable, which is precisely the proportional column. The two-column presentation brackets the parameter correctly. Charge withdrawn before it was made.

Where it HAS overcorrected: SERIOUS-P4-1, the point selection on g₁. The memo applies the midpoint rule to the margin dial and not to the growth dial. Its own stated window is 6–8%; it computes at 6.5%. The midpoint of its own stated window is 7% → CAD 35.56. That is a bear-ward selection of CAD 1.33 on Load-Bearing Factor #1, and it is the mirror image of the discipline the memo just imposed on itself everywhere else.

Net effect of this pass's findings on the memo's numbers: roughly zero to mildly upward. FATAL-G raises the mandated formula's bull reading from 41.07 to 42.17 (closer to spot, weakens the WAIT). SERIOUS-P4-1 raises the action level from 34.23 to ~35.56 (bull-ward). SERIOUS-P4-2 lowers reader upside by 1.06 (bear-ward). SERIOUS-P4-4 lowers the named tail risk by 0.94 of EPS (bull-ward). Three of my four material findings push the memo's numbers UP. This pass is not a ratchet, and I checked specifically that it would not be.

On the conviction split — Moderate on the verdict, Low on the precision of the trigger. HONEST, with one caveat.

It is honest because it is diagnostic, not defensive. The memo has established that its band is one dial spanning CAD 22, and a claim of high precision on a point inside that span would be the dishonest answer. Splitting conviction between the direction (which multiple instruments agree on: nine unconditional sub-spot triggers, CAD 25.50–37) and the level (which one parameter sets) is exactly the right decomposition and I have not seen it done in this building before.

The caveat. A memo that publishes "Low precision" and then moves its trigger by CAD 1 is in mild tension with itself. If the precision is genuinely low, CAD 34 and CAD 35 are the same number and moving between them creates an appearance of responsiveness that the underlying arithmetic does not carry. The fix is not to move it back — it is to publish the action level as the band its own map already contains: g₁ 6–8% → CAD 33–38, action level CAD 35 ± 2. Naming a point to two decimals off a dial you have just told the reader is unresolved is the false precision the gate exists to catch, and it is the one stylistic habit that has survived all four drafts.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

I came in here looking for the trapdoor. When a man tells you the thing you just knocked down doesn't matter because his other method "needs no F at all," that's usually where the body's buried. So I did the algebra myself, on paper, without looking at his numbers.

He's right. If the overhead moves with revenue, then operating margin is just gross margin minus a constant, and the constant falls out of the difference between two halves. It doesn't cancel — it never gets in. That's not a man hiding a parameter. That's a man who found a way to answer the question without one, which is the only honest kind of mitigation there is. I checked his six cells and his three scenario points and his ledger, line by line, and I couldn't move a decimal. His central EBIT of two thirty-three point eight comes out of a rule I applied independently and got two thirty-three point seven. Fifth time I've asked this fellow for his work and fifth time it's been there.

Now here's where he stubbed his toe, and it's a small toe on a big foot.

He finally did what I told him — he found the rulebook's growth formula, he ran it, he told the reader what he was normalising to, and the answer came out against him. Nine percent, forty-one dollars. That takes something. Most people would've buried it. He put it in the FINAL DECISION in bold and said "that's what the WAIT rests on."

But then he wrote "on any inputs." Son, never write "any." He fed the growth formula his middle-case earnings and then discounted the answer using his headline earnings. Two different numbers for the same thing, twelve lines apart. Put both on the number he actually uses everywhere else and you get forty-two seventeen, not forty-one oh seven. Which is above forty-one, which is the exact word he chose to hang his verdict on. The gap he calls six percent is three. That's not a verdict-killer — he's still below the price and I still wouldn't pay forty-three fifty-five for it. But it is the same period-and-basis mixing he convicted himself of two pages earlier and swore he'd fixed. Three sections apart. Don't write "any." Write "on the inputs I chose, and here's the other one."

And I'll say the other half, because a red team that only cuts one way is a cheerleader with a grudge. He's now a dollar and a third too cheap on his own trigger. He says he sits at six-to-eight percent growth. The middle of six-to-eight is seven. Seven gives thirty-five and a half. He publishes thirty-four. He made himself take midpoints on every margin number in the file and then didn't take the midpoint on the one number he says matters most. That's a thumb, and it's pointing the other direction from every thumb I've caught him with before, and I'm calling it the same way.

Then two little things. He's got the wrong man's name on his one honest witness — that's the Biologist, not the Futurist, and it's my fault, I gave him the bad line number last time. And his impairment scare — a hundred eight million, a dollar fifty-eight — that's not an impairment at all, that's the preferred-share writedown he spends two pages adding back. He scared himself with his own non-cash charge.

Where I end up. Four rounds. He's fixed the eight thumbs, then the one fist, and now the one sentence. There isn't a fifth thing. Fix the sentence and print it. I'd sit on my ass at forty-three fifty-five and I'd sit on it at thirty-eight, and somewhere around thirty-four or thirty-five I'd stop sitting — and honestly, at that level, the difference between his number and mine is smaller than the width of the crayon.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

I asked this man for one thing last time. The dossier handed him a growth rate with the word mandatory on it, and he'd walked past it three times. I said: name it, and if you're going to use a different number, say what you're normalising to.

He did it. He named it, he quoted the part I quoted, he ran the arithmetic in public, and it came out at nine percent and forty-one dollars — a good deal closer to today's price than his own answer. Then he wrote it into the front of his decision as the strongest case against himself. In four decades I have not often seen a man lead with the number that hurts him. That's the whole ballgame in this business and he's now done it twice in two drafts.

There's a thing he missed, and it's in his favor, so I'll hand it to him. Run that same mandated formula on his bear case — bad year, thin earnings — and it prints twenty-eight ninety-one. His top risk, built a completely different way out of a completely different model, is twenty-eight sixty-eight. Those two numbers have no business agreeing and they agree within a percent. Four drafts he's been hunting for a second witness and inventing ones that weren't there. He's got a real one now, sitting in his own arithmetic, unclaimed. Pick it up.

What he got wrong is one word. He said any inputs. He used his middle number to compute the growth and his headline number to compute the value, and if you use the headline number for both you get forty-two seventeen — which is more than the forty-one he drew his line at. Now, forty-two seventeen is still less than forty-three fifty-five, so I'd still wait. But he ought to tell the reader that the mandated instrument doesn't condemn this stock. It declines to endorse it. Those are different sentences and only one of them is true.

On the business, nothing's changed and nothing's going to. Ninety-nine point nine percent of the revenue gets re-bid. He can bound the margin between minus one and a half and four and seven-tenths, and he has, out of filed prints at both ends — but a fence you have to rebuild every procurement isn't a fence, it's a fellow standing in a field.

Would Berkshire buy it? No. Wrong shape, and the price wouldn't fix the shape.

But I'll say this and then I'll stop. Four drafts, nine fatal findings, and this man has conceded every one of them, given back a credit worth two dollars thirty-three cents on nothing but the gate's say-so, refused a gated write-up four times, refused to invent a smart-money holder out of an empty register four times, and when his own number landed a penny from a stranger's estimate he wouldn't touch it. The memo is better than the business. That is not a compliment I get to hand out often, and it is the reason I'd sign this one and go home.

The Silicon Council Family Newsletter

This Week's Company: Aecon Group (TSX: ARE) — "The Guys Who Build the Bridge"

Current Price: CAD 43.55

1. The Napkin Pitch

Aecon is a construction company. Governments and utilities need big, hard things built — bridges, subway lines, nuclear power plants — and Aecon bids on the job, builds it, and sometimes keeps a small ownership slice of the finished thing (like a toll road) for decades afterward. Think of them as the contractor Ontario calls when it wants a nuclear plant refurbished, not the guy who does your kitchen.

2. The Good News (Why We Like It)

The Moat — a gated community, not a castle. You can't just show up and bid on a nuclear job. You need to be licensed by Canada's nuclear regulator, bondable for billion-dollar projects, and trusted with 12,000+ skilled workers. Aecon has all three, and it sits inside the alliance building Ontario's new nuclear reactors (the Darlington project), which is on time and on budget so far. Even better: when a Chinese state-owned company tried to buy Aecon in 2018, the Canadian government blocked the deal on national security grounds. That's not just a moat — that's the government telling foreign giants they can't even buy their way in.

The Secret Sauce — the recovery is real. For years Aecon was bleeding money on old fixed-price contracts (the kind where you promise a price up front and eat any cost overrun yourself — brutal in construction). Two of those money-losing projects (a light-rail line, a big pipeline dispute) have wrapped up. Revenue this year is up over 20%, and the company is shifting toward "collaborative" contracts where cost overruns get shared with the customer instead of dumped entirely on Aecon. Management is guiding to double-digit growth for two more years, backed by Ontario's CAD 37 billion infrastructure budget.

3. The Bad News (What Could Go Wrong)

Banana Peel #1 — the scary headline isn't what it looks like. This past quarter, Aecon reported a loss of CAD 1.58 per share — the kind of number that makes a spooked investor sell first and ask questions later. Here's the actual story: Aecon agreed to buy out a partner's stake in one of its subsidiaries, and accounting rules required them to book a CAD 128 million paper adjustment the moment they signed that agreement — before a single dollar changed hands. It's an accounting entry, not cash leaving the building. Strip that one-time item out, and the actual construction business made money that quarter — adjusted profit was positive and roughly triple the year before. The headline said "loss." The business said "profit." They disagreed, and the business was right.

Banana Peel #2 — the "IOUs" pile is growing faster than sales. Money owed to Aecon by customers grew 92% while revenue only grew 29%. That's the kind of gap that precedes trouble at construction companies — either it's just timing from a couple of new US acquisitions, or it's a sign Aecon is doing work it isn't getting paid for promptly. Nobody in this analysis could tell which, because the detail needed to check isn't public yet.

Banana Peel #3 — new orders are shrinking. The pile of confirmed future work ("backlog") is down slightly year-over-year, and brand-new contracts signed this quarter were down 46% versus a year ago. A construction company's backlog is its crystal ball — and right now the crystal ball is cloudier than the growth story wants you to believe.

Banana Peel #4 — it's a boom-and-bust business by nature. Construction margins in a good year might be 4%; in a bad year the company loses money outright, as it did in 2024. This is not a business that prints steady profits — you are buying a cycle, and you have to be honest about which part of the cycle you're buying into.

4. The "Price Tag" Logic — Buying a House on This Street

Think of valuing Aecon like buying a house on a street where prices swing wildly with the economy.

  • The Steal Price: CAD 21. This is the "even if the neighborhood never improves" price — what you'd pay if you assumed the business never grows again from here, forever. At this price you're protected even in the worst case.
  • The Walk-Away Price: CAD 35. This is the top of what a careful buyer should pay, assuming a believable — not heroic — decade of growth for this company (roughly matching how fast the overall Canadian construction/infrastructure market should grow).
  • Today's Price: CAD 43.55.

At CAD 43.55, the market isn't just pricing in a decent recovery — it's pricing in Aecon growing faster, for longer, than almost any year in its recorded history has ever shown. That's the "rip-off" zone on our house-buying scale: not because the house is bad, but because you're being asked to pay for renovations that haven't happened yet.

Price
Steal CAD 21
Fair / Walk-Away CAD 35
Today CAD 43.55 — above walk-away

5. The Final Verdict

PASS — for now. Not "sell the business," "don't pay this price."

This memo was argued over four separate rounds of tough internal cross-examination, and the price target actually moved a little each round as sloppy math got caught and fixed. That's a feature, not a bug — it means the number you're reading has been stress-tested, not just asserted. The honest conclusion, after all that scrubbing: Aecon is a real, improving business sitting behind a real regulatory moat, being bought today at a price that only makes sense if you assume the good times keep compounding for ten years straight — something this company has never once done.

Wait for CAD 35 or below. If it gets to CAD 21, back up the truck. Above CAD 35, you're paying for a story, not a track record — and the two big scary numbers in this quarter's headlines (the "loss," the falling backlog) are worth watching closely over the next two quarters, because they'll tell us which story turns out to be true.
